mwg:efficient-background-processing:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Cache inactive SPA views with content-visibility: hidden so switching back needs no recomputation New capabilityPartialNewly Faster SPA view transitions via state caching · Work spent on what nobody can see
Unlock · brainstormTools with three to five tabs (edit / preview / settings, or several dashboard pages) can switch with zero wait while keeping scroll position and expanded state, without a framework keep-alive mechanism.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Add content-visibility: hidden (optionally with position: absolute to take no space) to inactive SPA views and remove it on switch; fall back to display: none via @supports not (content-visibility: hidden)
Blocker
Destroying the old view or hiding it with display: none both force the browser to rebuild or recompute full layout and paint when the user returns (quote: "Both approaches require the browser to recreate or recalculate the full layout and paint when the user returns to that view.")
Outcome
Partial — Hidden views skip layout and paint but keep their rendering state, so switching back is near-instant; unsupported browsers render all views at once unless they fall back to display: none (official claim)

Switching back and forth between tabs lays out each one from scratch every time. Hiding while keeping the cache spends almost no CPU on unseen views and reuses them on return. The cost is memory; see the next line.

Support content-visibility: Newly (2025-09-15; Chrome 108, Edge 108, Firefox 130, Safari 26)

mwg:faster-spa-view-transitions · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Caching every view exhausts memory; dynamic routes need an eviction strategy CorrectionPartialNewly Faster SPA view transitions via state caching · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Cache every view only when there are few, predictable views; apps generating many reports or routes implement an eviction strategy such as LRU and destroy old views at a memory threshold
Blocker
Unconditionally caching every view eventually bloats memory badly and may crash the browser on low-end devices (quote: "Doing so will eventually cause severe memory bloat and may crash the browser on low-end devices.")
Outcome
Partial — With eviction, frequently used views stay cached and memory use is bounded (official claim)

content-visibility: hidden keeps all DOM, listeners and state, so each report opened adds another copy. With unbounded routes, a long session eventually blows up the tab.

Support content-visibility: Newly (same as above)

mwg:faster-spa-view-transitions:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use blocking="render" so experiment scripts finish before first paint, replacing page-hiding anti-flicker snippets New capabilityPartialLimited Flicker-free client-side A/B testing · Content takes shape only after JS runs
Unlock · brainstormTheme, font-size and locale preferences stored in localStorage can be applied by a tiny render-blocking module before first paint, with no flash of default styles. Not supported in Firefox.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Put the experiment script in <head> with async (external) or type="module" (inline) plus blocking="render"; add blocking="render" to dynamically inserted variant stylesheets too; switch variants via a data attribute on <html>
Blocker
The script changes the DOM after the page starts building, so users see the original then a flash to the variant; traditional anti-flicker snippets hide the whole page with opacity: 0 until the script runs or about 4 seconds pass, sacrificing progressive rendering and allowing clicks on invisible content (quote: "This approach sacrifices progressive rendering, allows accidental clicks on invisible content, and introduces unnecessary paint cycles.")
Outcome
Partial — Supporting browsers don't paint until the variant is applied: no opacity, no timeout, no flicker, and HTML parsing isn't blocked; Firefox ignores the attribute, so feature-detect and fall back to the anti-flicker snippet there (official claim)

A/B tools have long had to pick a harm: don't block and it flickers, block and the page is blank for seconds and can be misclicked. blocking="render" gives scripts the same render-blocking behavior as stylesheets, delaying paint but not parsing. Firefox still needs the old snippet.

Support blocking="render": Limited (Chrome 105, Edge 105, Safari 18.2; not Firefox)

mwg:flicker-free-client-side-ab-testing · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

If a render-blocking script is slow or its CDN goes down, the page never paints CorrectionPartialLimited Flicker-free client-side A/B testing · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Keep only a small stub that applies the variant render-blocking (for example under 100ms execution), load tracking and analytics separately with async/defer; don't put blocking="render" on non-visual scripts; consider server-side splitting for performance-critical pages
Blocker
Rendering waits until the script executes, so a large or slow script directly delays first paint; a third-party CDN outage could block rendering entirely, with no developer-controlled timeout (quote: "If the experiment SDK is hosted on a third-party CDN, a CDN outage could block rendering entirely.")
Outcome
Partial — Shrinking what blocks to a minimal stub keeps first-paint delay under control; the CDN outage risk remains, and the guide only asks you to check the provider's uptime and monitor LCP/FCP (official claim)

Put blocking="render" on the whole experiment SDK and the flicker is gone, but every visit waits for it to download first; when the SDK host has an outage, the whole site is blank until the browser's own timeout.

Support blocking="render": Limited (same as above)

mwg:flicker-free-client-side-ab-testing:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Replace tracking pixels in unload with fetchLater(), letting the browser send whole-visit data when the user leaves New capabilityPartialLimited Reliably measure full-session analytics and telemetry · Background sends are unreliable or excessive
Unlock · brainstormA long-read page can keep updating 'read up to paragraph N, stayed this long', and the final state still arrives when the tab closes, without losing bfcache. Native in Chromium, polyfilled elsewhere.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Call fetchLater() as soon as data exists; when state changes, cancel the previous one with AbortController and reschedule with the full latest snapshot; the browser sends the last scheduled payload when the user leaves or closes the tab; where unsupported, use the guide's polyfill (built on fetch keepalive or sendBeacon())
Blocker
The old trick of creating an <img> pixel in an unload listener is very unreliable (especially on mobile) and makes pages ineligible for bfcache (quote: "Older technique like creating an <img> pixel in an unload event listener are notoriously unreliably (especially on mobile) and can negatively impact performance (by making pages ineligible for bfcache).")
Outcome
Partial — Supporting browsers reliably send the latest snapshot when the user leaves; Firefox and Safari use the polyfill, which differs only in detecting departure through visibilitychange (official claim)

Session length, whole-visit CWV and error logs are only known at the very end, and the old way forced a send on unload, which often never went out on phones and also hurt back/forward caching. fetchLater() lets the page keep updating 'what to send when leaving' and leaves the timing to the browser.

Support fetchLater: Limited (Chrome 135, Edge 135; not Firefox or Safari)

mwg:full-session-analytics · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

fetchLater() has a roughly 64KB per-origin quota; keep payloads small and wrap calls in try/catch CorrectionPartialLimited Reliably measure full-session analytics and telemetry · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Keep the payload minimal and wrap fetchLater() calls in try/catch to handle quota errors; don't use a ReadableStream as the body
Blocker
fetchLater() throws when the quota is exceeded, and a ReadableStream body also errors (quote: "DO NOT use a ReadableStream object for the request body, as that will error.")
Outcome
Partial — With a smaller payload and the error caught, quota problems are handled by your code instead of becoming uncaught exceptions (official claim)

A beacon rescheduled every 10 seconds carries the whole data set, which keeps growing until one call exceeds the quota and throws at the fetchLater() call. The guide asks you to shrink the payload up front and catch the error yourself.

Support fetchLater: Limited (same as above)

mwg:full-session-analytics:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use Long Animation Frames to find the scripts costing the most time on real users' devices New capabilityPartialLimited Identify heavy-running JavaScript · Performance measurement is skewed or blind
Unlock · brainstormBuild a 'third-party script bill' panel: how much main-thread time each external script consumes on real users' devices, to decide whether to replace a chat widget or ad SDK. Data comes from Chromium users only.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Observe long-animation-frame with PerformanceObserver (buffered: true), accumulate each entry's scripts, group by sourceURL, sum duration and rank the heaviest scripts (including third-party chat, video and analytics), then send them to an analytics service
Blocker
Lab data reproduces only a small subset of real usage and differs a lot from field data, making root causes of poor performance, especially slow interactions, hard to find; the JS Self-Profiling API has higher runtime overhead (quote: "field data is often very different to lab data, which only replicates a small subset of real user scenarios.")
Outcome
Partial — A lightweight API can name the scripts slowing frames in field data, whether one long run or many accumulated runs; only Chrome/Edge support it, but other browsers ignore it, and the guide says the issues found mostly apply to other browsers too (official claim)

The page stutters for users but runs smoothly on the dev machine, and profiling catches nothing. LoAF makes every frame over 50ms report which scripts ran and for how long, so you can tell in production which third party is dragging.

mwg:identify-heavy-scripts · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use Event Timing to split INP into three parts and find which part is slow first New capabilityPartialNewly Identify causes of poor INP · Performance measurement is skewed or blind
Unlock · brainstormAll four major browsers can report which button was slow and in which phase, so you can build a list of slow interactions ranked by target that points straight to the component to fix.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use onINP() from web-vitals/attribution to get the slowest interaction's inputDelay, processingDuration, presentationDelay and target, and send them back to analytics
Blocker
Interaction slowness depends on user actions, device and network, and lab data reproduces only a small subset of real cases, making it hard to diagnose; without splitting, it's easy to jump straight to the interaction handler (quote: "Lab data only replicates a small subset of real user scenarios so measuring the causes of slow INP in the field is essential.")
Outcome
Partial — Split into three parts, you can tell whether the problem is other code already running, this interaction's handler, or the rendering that follows; browsers without Event Timing can't measure INP (official claim)

INP is bad, so the first instinct is to optimize the button's click handler, when the real culprit is another script already running before the click, or the repaint afterward. The three-way split points the direction before digging in.

Support Event timing performance entries: Newly (2025-12-12; Chrome 76, Edge 79, Firefox 89, Safari 26.2)

mwg:identify-inp-causes · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use LoAF to separate style recalculation from layout time instead of heavy JS Self-Profiling New capabilityPartialLimited Identify causes of poor INP · Performance measurement is skewed or blind
Unlock · brainstormProduction slow-interaction reports can be labeled 'style recalculation' or 'forced layout' with the responsible script and function position attached, so whoever fixes it doesn't have to reproduce it locally first. Data from Chromium only.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Read forcedStyleDuration/forcedLayoutDuration (forced synchronous work inside scripts) and styleDuration/layoutDuration (frame rendering phase) from LoAF and report them separately; where the split fields are missing, fall back to the combined forcedStyleAndLayoutDuration and totalStyleAndLayoutDuration
Blocker
A full trace with the JS Self-Profiling API is heavyweight and can itself cause performance problems (quote: "A full performance trace using the JS Self-Profiling API is a heavyweight solution that is liable to cause performance problems.")
Outcome
Partial — A lightweight API distinguishes expensive selectors or broad invalidation (style) from geometry work or read/write layout thrashing (layout), which have different fixes; Chrome/Edge only, usable without a fallback (official claim)

Knowing that 'processing took long' isn't enough: style and layout are different diseases. Telling them apart used to require a full profile, but running a profiler in production slows the page itself. LoAF reports the two separately.

Support Long animation frames performance entries: Limited (Chrome 123, Edge 123; not Firefox or Safari)

mwg:identify-inp-causes:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use Speculation Rules to prefetch or prerender the next page before the user clicks New capabilityStalemateLimited Improve next page load performance · Loading priority is misplaced
Unlock · brainstormThe 'next step' of an online course, documentation or multi-step form can be prerendered so the page is already there on click, giving a static site instant navigation without becoming an SPA. Chromium only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Write prefetch/prerender rules in <script type="speculationrules">, preferring where document rules that pick links with href_matches/selector_matches, with eagerness (immediate/eager/moderate/conservative), optionally adding a tag the server can read from Sec-Speculation-Tags
Blocker
Missing: the guide only says starting to load the next page before the user visits is one of the most effective ways to improve load performance, without saying what concretely goes wrong otherwise (quote: "One of the most effective ways to improve page load performance for users navigating a site is to initiate loading the next page they're about to visit *before* they visit it.")
Outcome
Stalemate — Only Chrome/Edge support it; Firefox and Safari ignore the rules and navigate as before (official claim)

Rules let the browser fetch or render the next page ahead of time, when the user hovers a link or even right on arrival, and you can prefetch eagerly then prerender on stronger signals with moderate eagerness. It's pure progressive enhancement: unsupported browsers gain nothing.

Support Speculation rules: Limited (Chrome 109, Edge 109; not Firefox or Safari)

mwg:improve-next-page-load-performance · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't speculate state-changing URLs, and beware stale content on dynamic pages CorrectionPartialLimited Improve next page load performance · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
In document rules, use not to exclude URLs like /logout and add-to-cart, admin paths, and links marked .do-not-prerender or rel~=nofollow; use prerender and high eagerness cautiously on frequently changing dynamic pages
Blocker
Speculation can hit URLs that trigger state changes, and prefetched content on dynamic pages may be stale (quote: "DO NOT speculate URLs that likely trigger state changes, like /logout or /add-to-cart, and explicitly exclude them from your speculation rules if they are likely to be included in document rules.")
Outcome
Partial — With explicit exclusions, a site-wide document rule no longer touches state-changing URLs; for stale content the guide only asks for careful judgment (official claim)

A single href_matches: "/*" rule pulls in every same-origin link on the page, logout and add-to-cart included, so those URLs may be requested even though the user never clicked. An exclusion list is a required companion to a broad rule.

Support Speculation rules: Limited (same as above)

mwg:improve-next-page-load-performance:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use interaction-contentful-paint and soft-navigation to measure content that appears asynchronously after an interaction New capabilityStalemateLimited Measure interaction and soft-navigation paint timing · Performance measurement is skewed or blind
Unlock · brainstormAn SPA can rank LCP per route, or measure the real wait from 'press Search' to results appearing, to find the slowest navigations and API responses. Chromium only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Observe interaction-contentful-paint and soft-navigation with PerformanceObserver (buffered: true) to attribute content painted after fetch and SPA route LCPs back to the interaction that caused them; or use web-vitals v6 with { reportSoftNavs: true }
Blocker
LCP stops after the first interaction; INP only measures to the next frame, missing content rendered after fetch() and SPA route changes; watching URLs alone over-reports changes that aren't route changes (quote: "Just monitoring URL updates can over report URL changes that are not linked to route changes (for example, anchor link changes, multiple "redirects" for a single route change).")
Outcome
Stalemate — From Chrome/Edge 151, asynchronous paints and SPA navigations can be attributed back to interactions; Firefox and Safari can only skip these observers and keep the hard-navigation measurements, so the gap remains (official claim)

The user presses Search, the button changes color immediately (INP looks great), and results appear three seconds later. SPA page changes have no LCP at all. The new entries trace causality across fetch, await and setTimeout, measuring from the press to the content actually appearing. Chromium only for now.

Support Interaction contentful paint performance entries: Limited (Chrome 151, Edge 151; not Firefox or Safari); Soft navigation performance entries: same

mwg:interaction-paint-timing · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Match soft-navigation paints by interactionId and report duration CorrectionPartialLimited Measure interaction and soft-navigation paint timing · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
On each soft-navigation entry, seed LCP with getLargestInteractionContentfulPaint(); match later interaction-contentful-paint entries by interactionId, not navigationId; report entry.duration, not presentationTime directly
Blocker
In apps that render before calling history.pushState(), early paint entries carry the previous navigationId, so matching by it misses them; all timestamps are measured from the original hard-navigation origin, not the soft navigation start (quote: "If the application painted content *before* updating the URL (for example, rendering a view and then calling history.pushState()), those early interaction-contentful-paint entries were emitted before the soft-navigation entry and still carry the *previous* navigationId.")
Outcome
Partial — Matching by interactionId includes paints before the URL update and excludes later, unrelated interactions on the new route (official claim)

The intuition is 'same navigationId, same page', but frameworks often paint first and update the URL later, so the most important paint ends up filed under the previous page. Reporting presentationTime directly gives a number measured from when the whole app opened.

Support Same as above (Limited, Chrome/Edge 151 only)

mwg:interaction-paint-timing:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Long-lived SPAs can't rely on getEntriesByType('soft-navigation') CorrectionPartialLimited Measure interaction and soft-navigation paint timing · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Receive soft-navigation entries continuously with PerformanceObserver instead of querying performance.getEntriesByType('soft-navigation') afterward
Blocker
The browser buffer keeps only the first 50 soft navigation entries (quote: "DO NOT rely on performance.getEntriesByType('soft-navigation') to monitor long-lived single-page applications, as the browser's buffer is limited to the first 50 soft navigation entries.")
Outcome
Partial — With an observer receiving entries live, navigations after the 50th are still measured (official claim)

Admin panels and mail clients stay open all day and quickly pass 50 navigations. Querying the timeline afterward silently drops everything after that, with no error.

Support Same as above (Limited, Chrome/Edge 151 only)

mwg:interaction-paint-timing:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use content-visibility: auto containment to keep one column's changes inside that column CorrectionPartialNewly Optimizing interactions in complex layouts · Heavy work blocks the main thread
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Add content-visibility: auto and contain-intrinsic-size to self-contained layout units like Kanban columns or data-grid regions; where unsupported, fall back to contain: layout style paint via @supports not
Blocker
In complex layouts, changing one item (dragging a card, editing a cell) triggers chained style and layout calculations that reflow the whole page, causing dropped frames and high INP (quote: "performing a minor change to a single item—such as dragging a card or editing a cell—can trigger a chain reaction of style and layout calculations that forces the browser to reflow the entire page.")
Outcome
Partial — For visible columns the browser doesn't skip rendering; the benefit comes entirely from the layout, style and paint containment the property applies, so reflow stays within that column; unsupported browsers get a similar effect with manual contain (official claim)

Dragging a card from one column to another reflows the whole board and the drag stutters. With each column as a containment boundary, the browser knows changes inside can't affect the outside and reflows only that column.

Support content-visibility: Newly (2025-09-15; Chrome 108, Edge 108, Firefox 130, Safari 26)

mwg:interactions-in-complex-layouts · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use loading="lazy" on <video>/<audio> to defer poster and metadata requests until near the viewport New capabilityPartialLimited Lazy load video and audio · Loading more than is needed
Unlock · brainstormCourse catalogs, full podcast episode lists and product demo walls packed with media can lazy-load with plain HTML, no hand-written IntersectionObserver swapping data-src, and the first screen isn't held back by dozens of posters. Chromium only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Add loading="lazy" to below-the-fold <video> and <audio>, paired with preload="none" (user presses play) or preload="metadata" (duration and first frame fill in once in view); autoplay also waits until near the viewport to download and play
Blocker
Even with preload="metadata" or preload="none", the browser fetches the poster immediately, and preload="metadata" still issues range requests for every player on the page (quote: "Even when preload="metadata" or preload="none" is set on a <video>, the browser eagerly fetches its poster image immediately, and preload="metadata" still initiates range requests for media headers across every player on the page.")
Outcome
Partial — Supporting browsers defer poster, preload and autoplay until near the viewport; elsewhere the attribute is ignored, and with preload="none"/metadata the full file still isn't downloaded upfront, but posters and metadata are fetched immediately; fully deferring them needs your own IntersectionObserver (official claim)

A tutorial page with twenty videos, all preload="none", still fetches twenty posters on load. loading="lazy" lets media elements wait until scrolled near, like images. Chromium only for now.

Support Lazy-loading media: Limited (Chrome 150, Edge 150; not Firefox or Safari)

mwg:lazy-load-video-and-audio · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Lazy video needs width and height, lazy audio needs controls, or they never load CorrectionPartialLimited Lazy load video and audio · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Always give <video loading="lazy"> integer width and height; always add controls to <audio loading="lazy">
Blocker
Audio without controls has no visible box, can't intersect the viewport and never loads or autoplays; video without dimensions defaults to 300×150 and may never intersect and load, or cause layout shift when it loads (quote: "an <audio loading="lazy"> element without controls cannot visibly intersect the viewport and will never load or autoplay.")
Outcome
Partial — With dimensions and controls, the element has a real layout box to trigger loading and doesn't push the layout around when it loads (official claim)

Background music as an audio element without controls plus lazy, or a video sized only through CSS, and the lazy-load check never succeeds, so the media quietly never shows up.

Support Lazy-loading media: Limited (same as above)

mwg:lazy-load-video-and-audio:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

The window load event doesn't wait for lazy media CorrectionMissingLimited Lazy load video and audio · Wrong lifecycle assumptions
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Don't use the window load event to detect when lazy <video>/<audio> has loaded
Blocker
The load event considers only eager media; lazy media doesn't count even when it starts inside the viewport (quote: "The load event fires based only on eager-loaded media, even if a loading="lazy" media element is initially inside the visual viewport.")
Outcome
Missing — The guide only says not to rely on load and doesn't say what to use instead

Code that waited for load before initializing a player or measuring layout gets the timing wrong once lazy is added, because the media may not have started fetching when load fires.

mwg:lazy-load-video-and-audio:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't put loading="lazy" on above-the-fold or LCP video CorrectionPartialLimited Lazy load video and audio · Loading priority is misplaced
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Omit the loading attribute on the hero video (it defaults to eager) and add loading="lazy" only to media below the fold
Blocker
Adding loading="lazy" to above-the-fold or LCP video delays fetching both the poster and video data (quote: "DO NOT apply loading="lazy" to above-the-fold or Largest Contentful Paint (LCP) <video> elements, as it delays fetching both the poster image and video data.")
Outcome
Partial — The above-the-fold video loads eagerly as usual, with poster and initial media downloading immediately (official claim)

Adding lazy to every <video> at once means even the hero video, the first thing seen, waits for the browser's distance check before fetching its poster, slowing LCP.

mwg:lazy-load-video-and-audio:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use fetchpriority to tell the browser which image is the LCP and which hidden ones can wait New capabilityPartialNewly Optimize image priority · Loading priority is misplaced
Unlock · brainstormA homepage whose first screen holds a carousel, menu images and avatars can order downloads 'main image first, the rest later' with HTML attributes alone, without cutting the carousel to one image for LCP's sake.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Add fetchpriority="high" to the LCP candidate (usually the top hero image); add fetchpriority="low" to images technically above the fold but not initially visible (hidden carousel slides, mega-menu images)
Blocker
Browsers assign image priorities by heuristics, and the defaults may not match the page's LCP (quote: "Browsers use heuristics to assign loading priorities to images, but these defaults may not always align with your page's Largest Contentful Paint (LCP).")
Outcome
Partial — With explicit hints, critical images load faster and non-essential ones stop competing for bandwidth; unsupported browsers ignore the attribute and keep default heuristics (official claim)

To the browser, the hero image, the five other carousel slides and the promo image in a menu look about equally important and queue together. Marking the one that matters and the ones merely hidden gives bandwidth to the image actually seen first.

Support Fetch priority: Newly (2024-10-29; Chrome 103, Edge 103, Firefox 132, Safari 17.2)

mwg:optimize-image-priority · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

fetchpriority on the wrong images: too many highs, low on lazy below-the-fold images CorrectionPartialNewly Optimize image priority · Loading priority is misplaced
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Give fetchpriority="high" to at most 1–2 critical images and never combine it with loading="lazy"; ordinary below-the-fold images get only loading="lazy", not fetchpriority="low"; never lazy-load the LCP image
Blocker
Too many high images contend for the network and dilute the boost; lazy below-the-fold images also marked low don't load at normal priority when scrolled to (quote: "Only use fetchpriority="high" on at most 1-2 critical images to avoid network contention and diluting the priority boost.")
Outcome
Partial — Allocated by these rules, the boost concentrates on the real LCP image and scrolled-to images load at normal priority (official claim)

The instinct with a new attribute is to mark every important image high and everything else low; the highs cancel each other out and images below the fold crawl in when scrolled to. Priority is relative and only means something when few images are boosted.

Support Fetch priority: Newly (same as above)

mwg:optimize-image-priority:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use <link rel=preload fetchpriority="high"> so preloaded key images stop queuing at low priority New capabilityPartialNewly Optimize preload priority · Loading priority is misplaced
Unlock · brainstormLanding pages whose first screen is a video or a CSS background image can get the poster or background into the first slot just like an <img> hero, without changing the design for LCP's sake.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use <link rel="preload" as="image" fetchpriority="high"> for images the browser can't discover early but the page needs (video posters, hero images in CSS backgrounds); use fetchpriority="low" for preloads of secondary fonts or background video
Blocker
Preloads inherit the resource type's default priority, which for images is low (quote: "However, preloads inherit the default priority for the resource type and for images in particular this is low.")
Outcome
Partial — Key image preloads rank ahead of other preloads, start earlier and get more bandwidth; unsupported browsers still preload at the type's default priority (official claim)

You finally use preload to tell the browser about the hero background early, and it gets queued at low priority, losing to everything else. Adding fetchpriority="high" makes early discovery mean early download.

Support Fetch priority: Newly (2024-10-29; Chrome 103, Edge 103, Firefox 132, Safari 17.2)

mwg:optimize-preload-priority · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Too many preloads compete for bandwidth, and a wrong as wastes the preload CorrectionPartialNewly Optimize preload priority · Loading priority is misplaced
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Preload at most about 2 images and 2–3 essential fonts per page, give fetchpriority="high" to at most 1–2; write as and type correctly; prefer a static HTML <img> over preloading key images
Blocker
Too many preloads compete for bandwidth, and with an incorrect as the preload isn't used (quote: "DO: Limit the total number of preloads on a page to at most 2 images and 2-3 essential fonts to prevent bandwidth contention.")
Outcome
Partial — Limited in number and correctly typed, preloads go only to truly critical resources and are actually used by the page (official claim)

Preloading everything is the same as preloading nothing: it all fights for bandwidth. With a wrong as, what the preload fetched can't be used by the page, so it was fetched for nothing.

Support Fetch priority: Newly (same as above)

mwg:optimize-preload-priority:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use <script fetchpriority> so critical scripts download first and non-essential ones step aside New capabilityPartialNewly Optimize script priority · Loading priority is misplaced
Unlock · brainstormA page loaded with chat widgets, analytics, and A/B-testing scripts can get its interactive app script in first using only HTML attributes, without rewriting third-party scripts into deferred manual injection.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Add fetchpriority="high" to critical async/defer scripts that matter for INP; add fetchpriority="low" to analytics, ads, below-the-fold widgets, and parser-blocking scripts at the end of the body
Blocker
Browsers assign script priority by position and async/defer, so late-body scripts compete for bandwidth with more critical resources (quote: "Use fetchpriority="low" on parser-blocking scripts at the end of the body to prevent them from contending for bandwidth with more critical resources.")
Outcome
Partial — Critical scripts are queued first and non-essential scripts stop competing with key images and CSS; unsupported browsers ignore the attribute and use their own scheduling (official claim)

Analytics and the main app script are both async, so the browser can't tell which matters and fetches them together. Marking high/low lets the developer decide the order; it's progressive enhancement, so older browsers just keep their default.

Support Fetch priority: Newly (2024-10-29; Chrome 103, Edge 103, Firefox 132, Safari 17.2)

mwg:optimize-script-priority · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Give fetchpriority="high" to too many scripts and they all compete again CorrectionPartialNewly Optimize script priority · Loading priority is misplaced
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use fetchpriority="high" on at most 1–2 critical scripts; don't mark every <script>, only change the default when it's known to be sub-optimal
Blocker
Marking too many scripts high causes network contention and dilutes the boost (quote: "Only use fetchpriority="high" on at most 1-2 critical scripts to avoid network contention and diluting the priority boost.")
Outcome
Partial — Used sparingly, the elevated scripts really do go first (official claim)

Marking every script high is the same as marking none; only with a limit does priority mean anything.

Support Fetch priority: Newly (same as above)

mwg:optimize-script-priority:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use <?start>/<?marker> and <template for> to send the shell first and patch late content in the same stream New capabilityPartialLimited Out-of-order HTML streaming · Loading priority is misplaced
Unlock · brainstormPurely server-rendered pages (no front-end framework) can do "shell first, slow parts later": the article shows immediately while comments and related posts stream in once computed. Native support is Chromium-only.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Place processing instructions <?marker name> or <?start name>…<?end> (wrapping a loading fallback) early in the document; later in the same response stream send <template for="name"> and the browser swaps it in. Stream critical above-the-fold UI first and use placeholders for expensive blocks like comments, ads, and related content
Blocker
The guide only calls this an alternative to traditional top-to-bottom linear HTML processing, without saying when linear processing goes wrong (quote: "an alternative to the traditional "top-to-bottom" linear processing of HTML")
Outcome
Partial — The page shell ships immediately and database-heavy blocks are patched in when ready; Firefox and Safari need template-for-polyfill (official claim)

A slow block in the middle of the document used to hold up everything after it. With placeholders filled in later in the stream, order is no longer tied to document order; native support is Chromium-only for now.

Support <template for>: Limited (Chrome 150, Edge 150; not Firefox or Safari). HTML processing instructions: same (unsupported browsers parse <?marker> etc. as comments, which the polyfill still recognizes)

mwg:out-of-order-html-streaming · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use streamHTML() to pipe a fetch response straight into the DOM instead of waiting for a full JSON payload New capabilityStalemateNo browser support Out-of-order HTML streaming · Content takes shape only after JS runs
Unlock · brainstormIsland-style pages whose server returns HTML fragments could stream new content into the main region piece by piece after a link click, with no JSON API or client templates. No native browser support yet.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use the new HTML setter family (setHTML(), streamHTML(), streamAppendHTML(), 24 methods with Safe/Unsafe variants) and pipeTo(el.streamHTML()) a fetch response so the browser renders as chunks arrive
Blocker
The guide only says this replaces waiting for a full JSON payload and building the DOM by hand, without saying when that goes wrong (quote: "Stream large UI transitions in SPAs using streamHTML() instead of waiting for a full JSON payload and manual DOM construction.")
Outcome
Stalemate — The guide says HTML setters and streaming setters are not natively supported by any major browser yet; only html-setters-polyfill emulates the API (official claim)

SPA navigations wait for the whole JSON and then assemble the DOM by hand. Streaming setters would let HTML render as it arrives, but today it's only an API shape propped up by a polyfill.

Support HTML setter methods / HTML streaming setters: "not natively supported by any major browser yet."

mwg:out-of-order-html-streaming:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Put the live region around the progress text only, not the streamed content, and don't use role="alert" CorrectionWinWidely Out-of-order HTML streaming · Over-announcing
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Apply live-region markup only to the container for progress text like "Loading…"; don't wrap the whole content in a live region; don't use role="alert" for progress
Blocker
Wrapping the whole content in a live region makes screen readers read out everything patched in when streaming completes; role="alert" makes NVDA say "Alert" first (quote: "Do not wrap the entire content in a live region, as this will cause screen readers to announce the full "patched" content when streaming is complete, which can overwhelm screen reader users.")
Outcome
Win — Screen-reader users hear only a short progress notice instead of being flooded with the whole page (official claim)

Trying to tell screen-reader users the content has arrived ends up reading the entire block at once. Shrinking the live region to the progress text gives just enough notice.

Support aria-live: no support data in the guide; recorded as Widely following an existing line (mwg:language-model:3)

mwg:out-of-order-html-streaming:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't put the progress notice inside an aria-busy="true" panel CorrectionWinWidely Out-of-order HTML streaming · Announcements mistimed or unreliable
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Set aria-busy="true" on the containers being updated and back to false when done; keep the live-region notification outside those panels
Blocker
With the notification inside an aria-busy="true" panel, screen readers wait until all content has loaded before announcing it (quote: "Do not nest the live region-based notification inside panels with aria-busy="true", as this will prevent screen readers announcing the live region until after all of the content has been loaded.")
Outcome
Win — The progress notice is announced while loading is still under way (official claim)

A dashboard streams in several panels slowly, but the progress notice is wrapped inside a busy panel, so by the time it can be read it no longer means anything.

Support aria-busy / aria-live: no support data in the guide; recorded as Widely following existing lines

mwg:out-of-order-html-streaming:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't let focus vanish when a placeholder is replaced CorrectionWinWidely Out-of-order HTML streaming · Rebuilding the DOM loses state
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Preserve focus when replacing a placeholder that contains the active element; keep focus where it is during and after loading rather than moving it to new content (SPA navigation is the exception: move focus to the start of the new content when streaming completes)
Blocker
If a placeholder contains the focused element, focus is lost when it's replaced (quote: "make sure that focus is not lost if a placeholder containing the active element is replaced.")
Outcome
Win — The user's keyboard position isn't thrown away when content is patched in (official claim)

A keyboard user is sitting on a link inside a placeholder; the streamed content arrives, the whole block is swapped, and focus falls back to the top of the page. Preserve focus on replacement, or keep it somewhere that won't be swapped.

Support Uses plain DOM focus handling with no new feature; no support data in the guide, recorded as Widely (same treatment as :3 and :4)

mwg:out-of-order-html-streaming:5 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Reserve placeholder size up front so patched content doesn't shove the layout CorrectionWinWidely Out-of-order HTML streaming · Layout unexpectedly overflows or shifts
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Give placeholders stable dimensions; when the final size is unknown, use skeleton screens or CSS aspect-ratio to reserve space
Blocker
Placeholders without dimensions make the layout jump when content is patched in (quote: "Always provide stable dimensions for placeholders. If the final content size is unknown, use skeleton screens or CSS aspect-ratio to reserve space and prevent jarring shifts.")
Outcome
Win — With space reserved, patching no longer pushes surrounding content around (official claim)

Out-of-order streaming makes content arrive late, and late content without a reserved spot pushes the whole page down while the user is reading.

mwg:out-of-order-html-streaming:6 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use async/defer and inline critical CSS to stop render-blocking @import and big scripts in the head CorrectionWinN/A Performance · Loading priority is misplaced
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Inline above-the-fold CSS in <head> and defer the rest; give every non-critical script async or defer (type="module" defers by default); split stylesheets with <link media>; load third-party scripts with defer near the end of the page
Blocker
@import in CSS creates sequential request chains that delay the CSSOM; large non-critical scripts in the head stop DOM construction until they're downloaded, parsed, and executed (quote: "This halts DOM construction until the script is downloaded, parsed, and executed.")
Outcome
Win — Scripts no longer block DOM parsing and stylesheets no longer wait on each other in a chain (official claim)

One big script in the head and one @import in a stylesheet keep the page on a white screen. Inline what's critical, defer the rest, and the first paint doesn't wait for unimportant things.

Support No browser support data in the guide; recorded as N/A

mwg:performance · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Put the LCP image in the raw HTML, don't lazy-load it, and don't make JS or CSS its only source CorrectionWinN/A Performance · Loading priority is misplaced
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Make the LCP image appear in the raw HTML as an <img> (or <link rel="preload" as="image">) with fetchpriority="high"; never add loading="lazy" to above-the-fold images; don't use JS loaders to render the hero
Blocker
loading="lazy" on the LCP image deliberately delays the fetch until layout is done; rendering the hero with JS creates an HTML→JS→execution→image request chain (quote: "This purposefully delays the fetch until layout calculation is complete, severely degrading LCP.")
Outcome
Win — The preload scanner finds the LCP image as soon as it sees the HTML and starts downloading early (official claim)

When the biggest image is hidden in JS or marked lazy, the browser has to wait for layout or for scripts to run before it knows to fetch it. Written directly in the HTML, the scanner finds it immediately.

Support No browser support data in the guide; recorded as N/A

mwg:performance:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Priority is zero-sum: give too many resources high priority or preloads and they cancel out CorrectionWinN/A Performance · Loading priority is misplaced
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Reserve fetchpriority="high" for LCP images; fonts are already high priority so don't add it there; don't preload every font
Blocker
Elevating too many resources causes network contention and negates the boost; preloading all fonts starves other critical assets (quote: "Prioritization is a zero-sum mechanism. Elevating too many resources creates network contention and negates the benefit.")
Outcome
Win — Boosting only truly critical resources keeps the boost effective (official claim)

Everyone wants to be first, so everyone gets stuck together. High priority is a scarce resource; only one or two things can have it.

Support No browser support data in the guide; recorded as N/A

mwg:performance:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Yield with scheduler.yield() so the continuation goes to the front of the queue, not the back New capabilityPartialN/A Performance · Heavy work blocks the main thread
Unlock · brainstormSame as mwg:break-up-long-tasks: heavy in-browser work (parsing big files, building indexes) keeps the UI scrollable and cancellable, and long jobs don't get pushed far back just because they yielded.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Split JS work over 50ms and yield with scheduler.yield() (falling back to setTimeout wrapped in a Promise); move work over 250ms to a Web Worker
Blocker
Yielding only with setTimeout(..., 0) puts the continuation at the back of the task queue, so it can wait a long time behind other tasks (quote: "Standard setTimeout places continuations at the *back* of the task queue, potentially causing long delays if other tasks are pending.")
Outcome
Partial — scheduler.yield() puts the continuation at the front of the queue so work resumes quickly after yielding; unsupported browsers fall back to setTimeout and may still end up at the back (official claim)

You yield so user input can cut in, but with setTimeout your own second half also has to queue behind everyone else. scheduler.yield() yields without losing your place.

Support No browser support data in the guide; recorded as N/A (for this feature's support data see mwg:break-up-long-tasks)

Outcome

Change one number in a widget and the whole page reflows. Declaring containment stops recalculation at the component boundary.

Action + object

Blocker

will-change is a hint to the browser; sprinkled over the whole page it becomes a demand to give every element its own layer. Overdone, a performance trick ends up dragging performance down.

mwg:performance:9 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Service Worker caching needs per-type strategies, versioning, and limits CorrectionWinN/A Performance · Caching strategy misapplied

A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object

Blocker

Outcome

A Service Worker lets pages open without the network, but with the cache set up wrong users never see the new version. Choose a strategy per resource type and add versions and limits, and the cache becomes a help.

mwg:performance:10 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't use Cache-Control: no-store on non-sensitive pages, or you lose the bfcache CorrectionWinN/A Performance · Caching strategy misapplied
Who acts

Action + object

Blocker

Outcome

Writing no-store to "always get the latest" means every Back press reloads the page. With no-cache the content is just as fresh and Back is just as fast.

mwg:privacy:9 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use <install> / navigator.install() instead of beforeinstallprompt, which can only install itself New capabilityStalemateNo browser support Installing web apps · Install entry points are limited
Unlock · brainstormOne page could offer install buttons for *other* web apps, so a self-made catalog or toolbox page could install each small tool in one click. No browser supports it yet; track it for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Offer an explicit in-page install entry with the HTML <install> element or navigator.install(), including installing *other* apps (for example from an app catalog)
Blocker
The existing beforeinstallprompt can only install the current app; a catalog cannot offer installs for other apps (quote: "Unlike navigator.install(), this event cannot install a different app.")
Outcome

The guide funnels installation through browser-controlled consent UI, so sites cannot install silently. The new entry points would let a catalog install other apps in one click, but the guide states "<install> is not natively supported by any major browser yet", and the same goes for navigator.install(). Until support lands, the only fallback is the old event, which is exactly what cannot install across apps.

mwg:install-web-app · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Move origins with manifest migrate_from/migrate_to instead of making users uninstall and reinstall New capabilityStalemateLimited Migrating the origin of an installed web app · Migration loses user state
Unlock · brainstormA personal tools site can split tools.example.com/foo into its own subdomain or merge several small PWAs, and installed users follow with one "Update" click, no announcement explaining how to reinstall. Chromium-only for now, and data still has to be moved by hand.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Declare migrate_from in the new site's manifest (optionally behavior: "force"), authorize allow_migration at the old site's /.well-known/web-app-origin-association, and optionally add migrate_to to the old manifest; both origins must be same-site (same eTLD+1)
Blocker
Changing origin means users must manually uninstall the old app and install the new one, losing OS integration such as taskbar shortcuts and notification permissions (quote: "This process usually causes the loss of OS-level integration, such as taskbar shortcuts and notification permissions.")
Outcome
Stalemate — Supporting browsers hand over the installation (shortcuts, home-screen icons) automatically, but data and permissions don't move; Firefox and Safari don't support it, so the old site can only show a banner asking users to reinstall (official claim)

The product changes domain and installed users have to remove and reinstall it themselves, losing shortcuts and icons. Once both sides confirm each other, the browser swaps the shell; localStorage, IndexedDB, and permissions remain tied to the old origin and must be migrated separately.

Support Web app origin migration: Limited (Chrome 149, Edge 149; not Firefox or Safari)

mwg:migrate-web-app-origin · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use encapsulateKey() (MLKEM768-X25519) plus AEAD for post-quantum envelope encryption in the browser New capabilityPartialNewly Encrypt data client-side with WebCrypto · Sensitive data leaks
Unlock · brainstormA diary or notes app can encrypt each entry in the browser before upload so the server only stores ciphertext, with a non-extractable CryptoKey and no bundled crypto library. Falls back to classical exchange where the new algorithms aren't supported.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
The recipient generates a non-extractable key pair with crypto.subtle.generateKey('MLKEM768-X25519', false, …), stores it in IndexedDB, and exports the public key as 'raw-public'; for each message the sender calls encapsulateKey() to get a one-time shared key and ciphertext capsule, encrypts with ChaCha20-Poly1305 or AES-GCM, and sends { capsule, iv, ciphertext }
Blocker
The guide only says the hybrid KEM keeps the shared secret secure as long as either algorithm holds, without saying when classical-only key exchange goes wrong (quote: "it combines post-quantum ML-KEM (standardized in NIST FIPS 203) with classical 'X25519' so the shared secret remains secure as long as either algorithm holds.")
Outcome
Partial — Supporting browsers can do post-quantum envelope encryption locally; without SubtleCrypto.supports()/encapsulateKey() it falls back to classical X25519 + HKDF + AES-GCM (official claim)

End-to-end encryption in the browser no longer needs a crypto library, and the private key never leaves its CryptoKey. Browsers without the new algorithms fall back to classical key exchange behind the same interface.

Support Web Cryptography: Widely (2017-09-19); X25519 deriveBits: Newly (2025-02-06). The guide gives no support data for ML-KEM/encapsulateKey(); recorded as Newly, the lowest listed

mwg:client-side-encryption · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Bind unencrypted metadata into the AEAD tag with additionalData CorrectionWinWidely Encrypt data client-side with WebCrypto · Tampering goes undetected
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Pass unencrypted metadata such as record ID, recipient ID, or protocol version as additionalData when encrypting; use a fresh random 12-byte IV every time and never reuse an (iv, key) pair
Blocker
Without binding, tampering with the metadata next to the ciphertext goes unnoticed (quote: "Bind unencrypted metadata (such as a record ID, recipient ID, or protocol version) to the authentication tag via additionalData so tampering with either the ciphertext or the metadata causes decrypt() to reject with OperationError.")
Outcome
Win — If either the ciphertext or the metadata is changed, decrypt() rejects with OperationError (official claim)

The ciphertext itself is authenticated, but "who it's for, which record" sits beside it, so an attacker can move ciphertext under another record. Bind the metadata into the tag and moved ciphertext won't decrypt.

Support Web Cryptography (AES-GCM): Widely (2017-09-19)

mwg:client-side-encryption:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

New algorithms reject 'raw': write 'raw-public' / 'raw-seed' / 'raw-secret' explicitly CorrectionPartialNewly Encrypt data client-side with WebCrypto · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Always specify an explicit format in importKey()/exportKey() for MLKEM768-X25519, ML-KEM-*, and ChaCha20-Poly1305; import KEM private keys with only ['decapsulateKey']; use only the three algorithm names the guide lists
Blocker
Sticking with the legacy 'raw' format makes the new algorithms throw NotSupportedError (quote: "Modern WebCrypto algorithms (…) **reject the legacy 'raw' format** in importKey() and exportKey() with a NotSupportedError.")
Outcome
Partial — With explicit formats, import and export work; the classical fallback path still uses 'raw' (official claim)

Writing 'raw' out of habit makes the whole flow fail at step one on the new algorithms. The old and new paths use different format names and must be written separately.

Support Same as mwg:client-side-encryption; recorded as Newly

mwg:client-side-encryption:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Action + object

mwg:client-side-encryption:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Sign and verify in the browser with ML-DSA, no shared secret key needed New capabilityPartialNewly Sign and verify data with WebCrypto · Tampering goes undetected
Unlock · brainstormA static site can ship a signed release manifest that visitors' browsers verify after downloading files, without trusting the CDN in between; exported notes can carry a signature proving they weren't altered.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Generate a key pair with crypto.subtle.generateKey('ML-DSA-65', false, ['sign', 'verify']), export the public key as 'raw-public' for verifiers, and sign()/verify() messages, tokens, or release manifests; fall back to 'Ed25519' (or 'ECDSA' P-256) where unsupported
Blocker
The guide only says digital signatures guarantee integrity and signer authenticity without shared secret keys, without saying when other approaches go wrong (quote: "Digital signatures guarantee data integrity and signer authenticity without requiring shared secret keys.")
Outcome
Partial — Supporting browsers sign with post-quantum ML-DSA; others fall back to classical Ed25519 (official claim)

Confirming in the browser that a manifest hasn't been altered and really came from someone used to mean shipping a crypto library or deferring to the server. Now crypto.subtle does it directly, with a post-quantum option.

mwg:digital-signatures · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use context for domain separation so a signature for one purpose can't be replayed elsewhere New capabilityPartialNewly Sign and verify data with WebCrypto · Tampering goes undetected
Unlock · brainstormOne browser-side identity key can sign login challenges, comments, and file manifests without any of those signatures being reusable for another purpose, instead of managing a key per purpose.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Pass { name: 'ML-DSA-65', context } (0–255 bytes, identical on both sides) to sign()/verify(); when falling back to Ed25519/ECDSA, prepend a length-prefixed context header to the data yourself
Blocker
When one key pair is shared, a signature made for one purpose can be replayed in another part of the app; classical algorithms also silently ignore a context property (quote: "To prevent a signature created for one purpose from being replayed in another part of your application or protocol that shares the same key pair, bind the signature to a domain-separation context.")
Outcome
Partial — ML-DSA binds context natively, so verification fails under a different context; the classical path needs the manual prefix for the same effect (official claim)

If the same key signs both login challenges and release manifests, an attacker might pass one off as the other. Binding a purpose label keeps each signature valid only for its purpose; the classical fallback silently drops the label, so add it by hand.

Support Same as mwg:digital-signatures; recorded as Newly

mwg:digital-signatures:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Specify 'raw-public', 'raw-seed', 'spki', 'pkcs8', or 'jwk' when importing/exporting ML-DSA keys; import public keys with only ['verify'] and private keys with only ['sign']; don't use pre-standardization names like 'Dilithium'
Blocker
Sticking with the legacy 'raw' format makes ML-DSA throw NotSupportedError (quote: "'ML-DSA-44', 'ML-DSA-65', and 'ML-DSA-87' **reject the legacy 'raw' format** in importKey() and exportKey() with a NotSupportedError.")
Outcome
Partial — With explicit formats, import and export work; the Ed25519 fallback path still uses 'raw' (official claim)

Same pitfall as mwg:client-side-encryption:3: the new algorithms renamed the formats, and writing it the old way fails at the first step.

Support Same as mwg:digital-signatures; recorded as Newly

Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Detect with globalThis.SubtleCrypto?.supports?.('sign', 'ML-DSA-65') to choose between ML-DSA and Ed25519
Blocker

mwg:digital-signatures:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use targetAddressSpace so an https page can reach http devices on the LAN New capabilityStalemateLimited Local Network Access · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Unlock · brainstormSetup pages for smart appliances, 3D printers, or NAS boxes can be public https web pages that reach the device in the user's home, with no desktop app and no typing an IP into an http page. Chromium-only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Add targetAddressSpace: 'local' or 'loopback' to fetch()/Request calls to LAN devices (printers, IoT hubs, routers) or local companion apps; connect after the user grants the local-network/loopback-network permission
Blocker

Outcome
Stalemate — Supporting browsers allow it after permission and verify after resolution that the IP really is in the declared address space (aborting otherwise); Firefox and Safari don't support it (official claim)

Home devices only speak http, while web pages are always https, so the two can't connect. Declare "I'm connecting to the LAN" up front and the browser lets it through after asking the user, also checking the hostname didn't resolve to the public internet.

Tap favorite, sign in in place, the "Sign in" button disappears and focus goes with it, so keyboard users have to Tab from the header again. Put focus back on the favorite button and the action stays continuous.

Support Same as mwg:quick-sign-ins; recorded as Limited

mwg:quick-sign-ins:5 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use Connection-Allowlist to govern all outbound connections with one list, including channels CSP misses New capabilityStalemateLimited Restrict outbound connections with Connection Allowlists · Sensitive data leaks
Unlock · brainstormTools whose data should never leave the page, like a password generator, local notes, or a wallet, can declare in a response header that they only talk to themselves (or cut everything with () after load), so users or auditors can confirm they don't phone home. Chromium-only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Send Connection-Allowlist: (response-origin "https://api.example.com" …); report-to=… on document and worker-script responses with Reporting-Endpoints and Vary: Sec-Fetch-Dest; give Dedicated/Shared Workers the same list and the Service Worker a superset of all controlled documents; optionally send -Report-Only alongside to trial a stricter list
Blocker
CSP needs separate per-resource directives and doesn't cover speculative or non-Fetch channels like dns-prefetch, preconnect, or WebRTC, and compromised page scripts can still exfiltrate via worker.postMessage() (quote: "does not govern speculative or non-Fetch network channels such as <link rel="dns-prefetch">, <link rel="preconnect">, or WebRTC")
Outcome
Stalemate — Supporting browsers apply a single list to every outbound connection at the network layer, blocking redirects and WebRTC by default; it can't be polyfilled, so Firefox and Safari fall back to CSP and the channels CSP misses stay open (official claim)

With CSP in place, data can still slip out through DNS prefetch, preconnect, or WebRTC. Listing "only these destinations" at the network layer covers every channel at once; for now only Chromium enforces it.

Support Connection allowlists: Limited (Chrome 152, Edge 152; not Firefox or Safari)

mwg:restrict-outbound-connections · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Get the list syntax or defaults wrong and legitimate connections get blocked too CorrectionPartialLimited Restrict outbound connections with Connection Allowlists · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Write the header as an RFC 9651 Inner List (...) with space-separated items and unquoted response-origin and parameters; drop the trailing slash on origin-only patterns or use /*; rewrite ws(s):// as http(s)://; include external navigation targets (OAuth, payments); add ; redirects=allow or ; webrtc=allow when needed; wrap client fetch in try/catch to show a blocked state
Blocker
If a non-empty header fails to parse, the browser fails closed with an empty list and blocks all outbound connections; the trailing slash new URL() adds limits a pattern to the root path; by default even same-origin 3xx redirects are blocked (quote: "the browser fails closed with an empty allowlist and blocks **all** outbound network connections.")
Outcome
Partial — With correct syntax and patterns and the defaults opened deliberately, legitimate APIs, CDNs, navigations, and redirects work normally (official claim)

One missing pair of parentheses or one extra slash and every request on the page fails, or only the root path gets through. This header responds to mistakes by shutting everything, so generate it to spec and watch it in Report-Only first.

mwg:restrict-outbound-connections:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use native setHTML() to parse, sanitize, and insert in one step New capabilityPartialLimited Sanitizing untrusted HTML · Untrusted content parsed as HTML
Unlock · brainstormComment sections, Markdown note previews, and email viewers can setHTML() user HTML directly without downloading a sanitizer in supporting browsers, or use parseHTML() to build a clean preview before deciding to insert. Safari still needs DOMPurify.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Insert user-generated HTML with element.setHTML(html) (the default sanitizer removes scripts, event handlers, and anything outside its allowlist); pass new Sanitizer({ elements, attributes, replaceWithChildrenElements }) to restrict further; use Document.parseHTML() to parse without inserting; dynamically load DOMPurify only where unsupported
Blocker
<script> tags and on* attributes in user-generated HTML execute once they reach the DOM (quote: "The native **Sanitizer API** provides a built-in, browser-optimized way to strip dangerous content (like <script> tags or on* attributes) before it reaches the DOM.")

mwg:sanitize-untrusted-html · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Move sanitized nodes directly; don't serialize to a string and feed it back into innerHTML CorrectionPartialLimited Sanitizing untrusted HTML · Untrusted content parsed as HTML
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Move the result of Document.parseHTML() into the page with replaceChildren(...doc.body.childNodes)
Blocker
Serializing sanitized nodes to a string and re-parsing with innerHTML can reintroduce mutation XSS (quote: "Move the resulting nodes into the page rather than serializing them to a string and re-parsing it with innerHTML, which can reintroduce mutation XSS (mXSS).")
Outcome
Partial — Nodes are moved directly, so the sanitized result is never re-parsed and has no chance to mutate (official claim)

Turn clean content back into a string and parse it again, and the browser may parse it into a different structure; that's the gap attacks slip through.

Support Same as mwg:sanitize-untrusted-html; recorded as Limited

mwg:sanitize-untrusted-html:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Custom sanitizers delete content along with the element, and the DOMPurify fallback defaults differ CorrectionPartialLimited Sanitizing untrusted HTML · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
List wrapper elements whose text you want to keep in replaceWithChildrenElements; configure the DOMPurify fallback with ALLOW_ARIA_ATTR: false, ALLOW_DATA_ATTR: false, KEEP_CONTENT: false and allow #text; avoid replaceWithChildrenElements in configurations that need a matching fallback
Blocker
Elements not in elements are removed together with their content; DOMPurify by default keeps aria-*, data-*, and the text of removed elements, so the two paths produce different output (quote: "Elements that are not in elements are removed **together with their content**.")
Outcome

Allow only a few inline tags and a whole paragraph wrapped in <div> disappears; switch to the DOMPurify fallback in Safari and a pile of attributes shows up. The two have to be aligned on purpose.

mwg:sanitize-untrusted-html:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

mwg:sanitize-untrusted-html:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Add integrity to external scripts and stylesheets and pin them to immutable versioned URLs CorrectionWinWidely Secure external subresources with SRI, CORS, and Referrer Policy · Tampering goes undetected
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
At build or release time compute sha384- digests from known-good local files and put them in integrity on <script>, <link rel="stylesheet|preload|modulepreload">, and fetch(); use versioned or content-addressed URLs; list multiple digests when one URL may serve several valid payloads; for third-party scripts that update in place, skip integrity and use CSP or iframe sandboxing instead
Blocker
Referencing mutable URLs like latest/ exposes the site to silent supply-chain changes; computing the hash at runtime from the remote file trusts whatever the remote returns (quote: "rather than mutable floating URLs (latest/app.min.js), which expose your site to silent supply-chain changes and break SRI as soon as the remote file updates.")
Outcome
Win — If the bytes don't match the declared digest, the browser won't execute or apply them (official claim)

The file on the CDN gets swapped, and the site loads and runs it anyway. Pin the version and write the hash, and any change in content is blocked.

Support The guide says subresource-integrity and referrer-policy on <link rel="stylesheet"> are "widely available across all browsers"; recorded as Widely

mwg:secure-external-subresources · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

If there's integrity, there must be crossorigin, or the resource is simply blocked CorrectionWinWidely Secure external subresources with SRI, CORS, and Referrer Policy · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When setting integrity on a cross-origin resource, also set crossorigin="anonymous" (or use-credentials) and make sure the server sends CORS headers
Blocker
Without crossorigin, the browser fetches in no-cors mode, gets an opaque response whose hash can't be verified, and blocks the resource (quote: "Omitting crossorigin causes the browser to fetch the resource in no-cors mode (producing an opaque response that cannot be hash-verified) and block the resource.")
Outcome
Win — A CORS response can be hash-verified and loads normally (official claim)

You add an integrity check and the file doesn't load at all, because the browser can't see the content to compare.

Support Same as mwg:secure-external-subresources; recorded as Widely

mwg:secure-external-subresources:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use CSS url() integrity() / cross-origin() / referrer-policy() to verify resources loaded from stylesheets New capabilityStalemateLimited Secure external subresources with SRI, CORS, and Referrer Policy · Tampering goes undetected
Unlock · brainstormA design system published from a CDN can pin hashes on its fonts, icon sprites, and theme-token files right in the CSS, without splitting @imports into many <link>s for SRI. Only Chromium verifies for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Inside url("…") for @import, @font-face src, background-image, mask-image, etc., add cross-origin(anonymous) referrer-policy(no-referrer) integrity("sha384-…"); quote the URL and use each modifier only once
Blocker
background-image, content, border-image-source, and @import fetch in no-cors mode by default, and the opaque response can't be hash-verified (quote: "background-image, content, border-image-source, and @import default to no-cors mode (producing an opaque response that cannot be hash-verified)")
Outcome
Stalemate — Supporting browsers can verify fonts, images, and imported stylesheets from CSS; integrity() is Chromium-only and Firefox supports none of the three modifiers, so elsewhere you can only swap @import for <link>, and fonts and images in CSS remain unverified (official claim)

Every script in the HTML has SRI, but fonts and @imports pulled from a CDN inside stylesheets couldn't be verified. Now you can put the hash inside url(), though for now only Chromium checks it.

mwg:secure-external-subresources:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't put an unverified fallback in the same @font-face src list CorrectionPartialLimited Secure external subresources with SRI, CORS, and Referrer Policy · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Do progressive enhancement with **separate** src: declarations: first a plain url(), then a version with only cross-origin()/referrer-policy(), and finally the version with integrity()
Blocker
With verified and unverified URLs in one comma-separated src, a tampered font from the CDN fails the check, is treated as a load failure, and the browser falls back to the second, unverified URL (quote: "in a single src descriptor bypasses Subresource Integrity at runtime")
Outcome
Partial — Supporting browsers overwrite the unverified declarations at parse time and block tampered fonts at runtime (official claim)

An extra fallback for compatibility turns into a back door: once verification fails, the browser obligingly loads the same file unverified.

mwg:secure-external-subresources:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Set referrerpolicy="no-referrer" on external resources, with a document-level baseline policy CorrectionWinWidely Secure external subresources with SRI, CORS, and Referrer Policy · Sensitive data leaks
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object

Blocker

Outcome

mwg:secure-external-subresources:5 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Replace script domain allowlists with a nonce plus 'strict-dynamic' CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Defenses configured into no-ops
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use a fresh per-request nonce (script hashes for static HTML) with 'strict-dynamic' and 'report-sample' in CSP script-src, alongside object-src 'none', base-uri 'none', and frame-ancestors; let third-party analytics load their dependencies through 'strict-dynamic' instead of allowlisting their domain
Blocker
Domain allowlists such as https: or script-src https://cdn.example.com as the primary defense can be bypassed through open redirects, JSONP endpoints, or dependency injection on the allowed origin (quote: "they are easily bypassed by open redirects, JSONP endpoints, and dependency injection on the allowed origin.")
Outcome
Win — Only scripts carrying this response's nonce (and what they load) can run, so a hole on an allowlisted domain is no longer a free pass; the guide calls this the primary mechanism to prevent XSS (official claim)

An allowlist looks strict but really trusts every endpoint on that domain. With a nonce, what you trust is the handful of <script> tags you wrote into this very response.

mwg:security · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Hand nonces to your own scripts in the template, don't stamp them on with a regex CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Defenses configured into no-ops
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Generate a nonce per request in the server or SSR middleware with crypto.getRandomValues(), put it in the Content-Security-Policy header and pass it into the template rendering context so only developer-authored <script> tags receive it
Blocker
Adding nonces to every <script> in already-rendered HTML with a regular expression cannot tell developer scripts from ones injected via reflected or stored HTML, so it authorizes the attacker's scripts (quote: "authorizing attacker-injected scripts under script-src 'nonce-...'.")
Outcome
Win — Nonces go only to trusted tags at render time; injected <script> tags never get one (official claim)

To ship CSP quickly, some sites scan the output and stamp every <script>. That hands CSP's core decision to a regex, and a regex can't tell friend from foe.

mwg:security:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Keep untrusted strings out of dangerous sinks and enforce it at runtime with Trusted Types CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Untrusted content parsed as HTML
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Set text with textContent/innerText; insert untrusted HTML with setHTML(); build elements with document.createElement or DOMParser instead of concatenating HTML strings; add require-trusted-types-for 'script' to CSP and route necessary sink writes through a named trustedTypes.createPolicy(), never a pass-through policy
Blocker
Untrusted or unsanitized input written to the DOM through innerHTML or setHTMLUnsafe becomes DOM XSS; a pass-through policy that returns input unchanged neutralizes Trusted Types (quote: "**DO NOT**: Use innerHTML or setHTMLUnsafe with untrusted or unsanitized input.")
Outcome

innerHTML, eval, and string setTimeout are the list you grep for. A source-level rule depends on people following it; Trusted Types turns the same rule into a runtime error.
