mwg:security:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Start HSTS with a short max-age and raise it gradually CorrectionPartialN/A Web security · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Send Strict-Transport-Security: max-age=31536000; includeSubDomains; preload to force HTTPS; in production start with a max-age of about 300 seconds and raise it step by step to a year
Blocker
Sending a long max-age from day one means a misconfiguration makes the site unreachable in every browser that saw the header until the cache expires (quote: "A misconfigured HSTS with a long max-age can render the site permanently inaccessible until the cache expires in every browser that saw it.")
Outcome
Partial — Starting short limits a lockout to the short cache lifetime; once raised, the same risk returns (official claim)

HSTS can't be taken back once sent; browsers remember it for a full year. So send five minutes first, make sure every subdomain keeps up with HTTPS, then lengthen it.

Support HSTS: the guide gives no browser support data; recorded as N/A

mwg:security:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use the __Host- prefix and HttpOnly on session cookies CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Sensitive data leaks
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Name single-domain first-party session cookies __Host-session (requires Secure, Path=/, no Domain), fall back to __Secure- otherwise; set HttpOnly on session and auth cookies and explicitly set SameSite=Lax
Blocker
A session cookie without HttpOnly can be read by injected client-side scripts (quote: "Set HttpOnly (httpOnly: true) on session and authentication cookies so they cannot be read by injected client-side scripts")
Outcome
Win — HttpOnly keeps injected scripts away from the session cookie; the __Host- prefix protects against same-site and network attackers (official claim)

Once a page has XSS, any script that can read cookies can walk off with the session. Make the cookie unreachable to scripts and a big chunk of the damage disappears.

Switch apps embedded in third-party pages to partitioned cookies New capabilityMissingN/A Web security · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Unlock · brainstormWhen third-party cookies got blocked, comment boxes, support chats, and payment widgets embedded in other sites forgot their users → now partitioned cookies keep sign-in and preferences within each site without turning the widget into a cross-site tracker. Same CHIPS as mwg:privacy:7

Action + object
When the app is embedded as an iframe in third-party contexts, set cookies with SameSite=None; Secure; Partitioned
Blocker

mwg:security:6 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Pick a frame-ancestors tier per embedding need, and drop X-Frame-Options for cross-origin partners CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
No framing: frame-ancestors 'none' plus X-Frame-Options: DENY; same-origin only: 'self' plus SAMEORIGIN; specific cross-origin partners: only frame-ancestors 'self' https://trusted-partner.com, omitting X-Frame-Options
Blocker
Still sending X-Frame-Options: DENY or SAMEORIGIN when cross-origin embedding is needed makes clients that evaluate XFO without frame-ancestors block the embed (quote: "DENY or SAMEORIGIN would block the embed in any client that evaluates X-Frame-Options without frame-ancestors")
Outcome

mwg:security:7 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Get cross-origin isolation with Document-Isolation-Policy instead of adding CORP to every subresource New capabilityStalemateLimited Web security · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Unlock · brainstormUsing SharedArrayBuffer used to require every third-party image, font, and video host to add CORP → now in-browser Wasm thread tools (video transcoding, local image processing) can enable isolation while still embedding outside assets. Mainly Chrome for now
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Only isolate when SharedArrayBuffer is needed (e.g. WebAssembly threads); in Chromium prefer Document-Isolation-Policy: isolate-and-credentialless, which strips credentials from non-CORS cross-origin fetches instead of blocking them; use isolate-and-require-corp when blocking is wanted
Blocker
The cross-browser path (COOP: same-origin plus COEP: require-corp) requires every embedded subresource to send a CORP header or the browser won't load it (quote: "Every embedded subresource (images, styles, external media) MUST serve an explicit Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy header or the browser will prevent it from loading.")
Outcome
Stalemate — Document-Isolation-Policy lets cross-origin resources without CORP load (without credentials); but it is supported mainly in Chrome 142+ and other vendors haven't shown interest, so cross-browser isolation still goes through COEP (official claim)

Turning on isolation for SharedArrayBuffer used to mean every third-party image needed its host to add a header, or it broke. The new policy loads them without cookies instead, but only in Chrome for now.

mwg:security:8 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use Fetch Metadata to reject cross-site requests before authentication CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Sensitive data leaks
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
On the server check Sec-Fetch-Site/Sec-Fetch-Mode/Sec-Fetch-Dest, reject cross-site requests to non-navigational endpoints before any authentication or authorization check; add Vary: Sec-Fetch-Dest, Sec-Fetch-Mode, Sec-Fetch-Site; drop same-site from the allowlist if any subdomain hosts user content
Blocker
Rejecting only after authentication leaks timing information about whether a resource or session exists; without Vary, an intermediate CDN may serve cached responses to attackers (quote: "Reject disallowed requests *before* authentication or authorization checks, so the response does not leak timing information about whether a resource or session exists.")
Outcome
Win — Rejecting early leaves no timing gap, and Vary makes caches separate responses by Fetch Metadata (official claim)

The cross-site request gets blocked, but if the block comes after looking up the account, an attacker can infer existence from response time alone. Reject first, then look up, and keep caches separated.

Support Fetch Metadata: the guide gives no browser support data (its example lets legacy browsers without Sec-Fetch-Site through); recorded as N/A

mwg:security:9 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Credentialed CORS must echo a specific origin, not * CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Validate the Origin request header on the server and set Access-Control-Allow-Origin dynamically to that origin; handle OPTIONS preflight first
Blocker
Setting Access-Control-Allow-Origin to * when Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true is required makes the browser reject the response (quote: "Use wildcard * for Access-Control-Allow-Origin if Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true is required — the browser will reject the response.")
Outcome
Win — Echoing a validated origin makes credentialed cross-origin reads work without opening up to every origin (official claim)

CORS is a grant, not a defense, and the usual mistake is granting too much. The wildcard-plus-cookies combo is one the browser simply refuses to read.

Support CORS: the guide gives no browser support data; recorded as N/A

mwg:security:10 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Send a complete Clear-Site-Data on logout CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Sensitive data leaks

Action + object
Have the logout endpoint respond with Clear-Site-Data: "cookies", "storage", "cache", all three directives
Blocker
Omitting any one leaves active session cookies, Web Storage state, or cached authenticated pages in the browser (quote: "omitting any of these directives leaves active session cookies, Web Storage state, or cached authenticated pages behind.")
Outcome
Win — All three kinds of data are cleared together and the session fully ends (official claim)

The logout button was pressed and the server session deleted, yet the browser cache still holds the previous account page. The next person at a shared computer just presses Back.

mwg:security:11 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use SRI only on immutable, versioned assets CorrectionWinN/A Web security · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Add integrity (preferring sha384, sha512, or sha256) with crossorigin="anonymous" to third-party scripts and stylesheets, with the CDN sending Access-Control-Allow-Origin; only for pinned-version URLs
Blocker
Putting SRI on dynamic or unversioned assets means the hash stops matching on the next silent update and the script fails to run (quote: "Use SRI for dynamic or unversioned assets — silent updates will cause script execution to fail.")
Outcome
Win — SRI pinned to fixed versions never breaks because upstream changed (official claim)

SRI exists precisely to refuse changed content, so binding it to an auto-updating file means the site breaks itself on the upstream's next release day.

Support SRI: this guide gives no browser support data; recorded as N/A (the secure-external-subresources guide records Widely)

mwg:security:12 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use Trusted Types so dangerous sinks only accept policy-made objects New capabilityPartialNewly Prevent DOM-based XSS attacks with Trusted Types · Untrusted content parsed as HTML
Unlock · brainstormConfirming no innerHTML slipped through used to rely on code review and grep → now comment sections, Markdown previews, and CMS editors can have the browser guarantee across all four engines that any unsanitized write throws and gets reported
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Define sanitizing policies with trustedTypes.createPolicy() (delegating to DOMPurify or the app's escaping rules), convert to TrustedHTML/TrustedScriptURL before writing to sinks such as innerHTML, document.write(), <script src>, and Worker(); enforce with CSP require-trusted-types-for 'script' and restrict policy names with the trusted-types directive
Blocker
These DOM APIs accept raw strings by default, so a string carrying malicious script from an untrusted source gets executed (quote: "If a string contains malicious script from an untrusted source, it can be executed.")
Outcome
Partial — Under enforcement, string writes to sinks that skip a policy throw, and unlisted policies can't be created; setHTML() and Document.parseHTML() sanitize natively and need no policy. Trusted Types only became Baseline in 2026-02; older browsers run the sanitizing logic through a tinyfill but get no sink-level enforcement (official claim)

Asking everyone not to use innerHTML is one thing; guaranteeing no code path ever pushes a raw string into it is another. Trusted Types hands the latter to the browser: any stray write throws.

Support Trusted Types: Newly available (2026-02-24). Supported by: Chrome 83, Edge 83, Firefox 148, Safari 26

mwg:trusted-types · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Collect violations in Report-Only first; enforce Trusted Types only once they reach zero CorrectionPartialNewly Prevent DOM-based XSS attacks with Trusted Types · Legitimate connections blocked by security rules
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
First confirm the framework and third-party libraries produce TrustedHTML/TrustedScript; send Content-Security-Policy-Report-Only: require-trusted-types-for 'script', optionally registering a temporary default policy that logs where strings still arrive; switch to the enforcing Content-Security-Policy once reports drop to zero
Blocker
Enforcing while some sink writes aren't migrated, or while the framework or libraries don't produce Trusted Types, breaks the application (quote: "it carries a risk of site breakage if every sink write hasn't been migrated.")
Outcome
Partial — Report-Only logs without blocking, so sinks can be fixed one by one without breaking the site; enforcement is still impossible if the framework won't cooperate (official claim)

Trusted Types protects by always throwing, and the same trait can take the site down the moment it's switched on. Observe, fix, and only then enforce.

Support Same as mwg:trusted-types; recorded as Newly

mwg:trusted-types:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Character escaping is not an HTML sanitizer CorrectionPartialNewly Prevent DOM-based XSS attacks with Trusted Types · Defenses configured into no-ops
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use element.textContent (not a Trusted Types sink) when only plain text is needed; use a full sanitizer (DOMPurify or element.setHTML()) when accepting markup or attributes; never interpolate escapePolicy.createHTML() output into another HTML template
Blocker
Interpolating character-escaped strings into unquoted attributes or URL attributes like href leaves javascript: URLs un-neutralized by entity escaping (quote: "a URL attribute (<a href="${escaped}">, where javascript: URLs are not neutralized by entity escaping)")
Outcome

An escaping policy makes Trusted Types stop complaining, so it looks like the check passed. But &lt; can't save you from javascript:alert(1): it has no angle brackets to begin with.

mwg:trusted-types:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Replace string prefix checks with Origin.from() plus isSameOrigin() New capabilityPartialLimited Compare and validate web origins and sites · Verifying the wrong identity
Unlock · brainstormEvery postMessage receiver used to parse URLs and handle default ports itself → now embedded checkouts, sign-in popups, and cross-window widgets can hand the event straight to Origin.from() and compare in one line. Not yet in Firefox
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
For privileged actions such as cross-window postMessage commands or deciding whether to attach credentials, convert both sides to Origin objects with Origin.from() (MessageEvent, <a>, and URL can be passed directly) and compare scheme, host, and port with isSameOrigin(); wrap calls in try/catch so invalid URLs and "null" return false
Blocker
A startsWith() prefix check is bypassed by https://app.example.com.attacker.example; full URLs with paths or explicit default ports don't match under string equality (quote: "Checking url.startsWith('https://app.example.com') is bypassed by 'https://app.example.com.attacker.example'.")
Outcome
Partial — Comparison happens after the browser's URL parser normalizes both sides, so prefix spoofing and port spelling no longer cause misjudgments; Firefox lacks support and must fall back to comparing .origin from new URL() after rejecting "null" (official claim)

Prefix checks always pass in tests, because nobody tests with app.example.com.attacker.example. With structured origin objects you compare the parsed triple, not whether strings look alike.

Support Origin: Limited availability. Supported by: Chrome 145, Edge 145, Safari 26.5. Unsupported in: Firefox

mwg:validate-origins · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use isSameSite() for cross-subdomain redirects and links New capabilityPartialLimited Compare and validate web origins and sites · Verifying the wrong identity
Unlock · brainstormCorrect same-site checks used to mean shipping a Public Suffix List to the front end → now services spread across subdomains (billing, help center, main site) can safely allow redirects between each other without being spoofed by neighbors on the same public suffix. Not in Firefox
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When validating post-login ?returnTo= targets or classifying links as internal vs. external, convert with Origin.from() and call isSameSite() for a schemeful same-site comparison
Blocker
Splitting hostnames on . to decide same-site gets multi-label public suffixes like .co.uk and .github.io wrong and ignores http vs. https (quote: "Splitting hostnames on . to check same-site relationships fails on multi-label public suffixes (such as .co.uk or shared hosting domains like .github.io) and ignores scheme mismatches (http: vs. https:).")
Outcome
Partial — isSameSite() uses the browser's built-in Public Suffix List, so tenant-a.github.io and tenant-b.github.io count as different sites and http downgrades are rejected; in unsupported browsers URL has no PSL access, so you can only match domains you control or ship a PSL library (official claim)

Take the last two labels of a.co.uk and b.co.uk and both are co.uk, so they look like one site. The right answer was always inside the browser; it just wasn't exposed.

Support Same as mwg:validate-origins; recorded as Limited

mwg:validate-origins:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Tell sandboxed iframes apart with Origin.from(event) New capabilityPartialLimited Compare and validate web origins and sites · Verifying the wrong identity
Unlock · brainstormTelling several sandboxed iframes on one page apart used to require event.source or a separate MessageChannel → now plugin systems, user code previews, and multiple third-party widgets can each live in a sandbox while their messages still map reliably to the sender. Not in Firefox
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When receiving messages from a sandboxed iframe (sandbox="allow-scripts"), extract the underlying opaque origin with Origin.from(event), pin it once at initialization from the expected iframe, then compare with isSameOrigin(); use new Origin() as a match-nothing sentinel before pinning; reject redirect targets whose origin.opaque is true
Blocker
Sandboxed iframes and data: URLs all serialize event.origin to the string "null", so comparing with === treats two unrelated opaque origins as the same (quote: "Comparing two serialized runtime origins with originA === originB falsely equates two unrelated opaque contexts ("null" === "null").")
Outcome
Partial — Messages from the same sandboxed iframe match each other and different iframes don't; in unsupported browsers event.origin is always "null", so check event.source or use a dedicated MessageChannel handed over at initialization (official claim)

Locking untrusted code inside a sandboxed iframe is a good idea, but every sandbox signs its mail "null". The new API lets the browser say whether this message came from the same sandbox as the last one.

Support Same as mwg:validate-origins; recorded as Limited

mwg:validate-origins:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Both sides of the Origin API must be Origin objects, and calls need try/catch CorrectionPartialLimited Compare and validate web origins and sites · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Run both sides through Origin.from() before comparing; get the current context's origin with Origin.from(self); fall back to parsing event.origin for synthetic test MessageEvents; wrap every call on runtime input in try/catch returning false
Blocker
Passing a raw string to isSameOrigin(), passing Location/Document to Origin.from(), or synthesizing events with new MessageEvent() in unit tests all throw a TypeError (quote: "passing a raw string throws a TypeError.")
Outcome
Partial — Written this way, invalid URLs, "null" strings, and unsupported objects safely return false instead of throwing uncaught errors (official claim)

The new API is stricter than strings, strict enough to throw on the wrong type. When an origin check throws, the whole message handler stops, so every call needs a fallback.

mwg:validate-origins:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Confirm email ownership without sending mail, using autocomplete="email-verification-token" New capabilityStalemateLimited Verify email address ownership · Sign-in interrupts the user
Unlock · brainstormVerifying an email used to mean switching to the inbox and copying a code → now sign-up, checkout, and newsletter subscription can finish verification on the same page, almost frictionless for users of participating providers like Gmail. Chrome origin trial only for now
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Put an autocomplete="email" field and a hidden autocomplete="email-verification-token" input carrying a server-side nonce in the same <form>; the browser checks the domain's DNS record, obtains an Email Verification Token from the email provider, binds it to the origin and nonce, and submits it; if the backend verifies it, skip the one-time code or magic link
Blocker
The traditional one-time code or magic link makes users leave the page, wait for mail, and copy codes, causing drop-off and phishing risk, and tells the email provider which site the user is visiting (quote: "This forces users to leave your page, wait for email delivery, and copy codes—introducing friction, drop-off, and phishing risk, while also revealing to the email provider which relying party (RP) the user is visiting.")
Outcome
Stalemate — With a supporting browser, a signed-in provider session, and a domain that publishes the DNS record, ownership is verified on the spot without the provider learning which site asked; but it currently requires an origin trial, and otherwise the field submits empty and you fall back to sending a verification email (official claim)

Go check your inbox and type the six digits back is where sign-ups leak most. The browser is already signed into the mailbox, so the new protocol lets it present proof directly; when unsupported, the old flow takes over untouched.

Support Email Verification API: the guide gives no Baseline data, only says to enable an origin trial and mentions Chrome Origin Trial builds; recorded as Limited (Chrome origin trial only)

mwg:verify-email-ownership · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Keep the verification nonce on the server and use it once CorrectionPartialLimited Verify email address ownership · Verifying the wrong identity
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Generate a nonce of at least 128 bits on the server for every form render, store it in server-side session state keyed by an HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite=Lax session cookie; serve the page with Cache-Control: no-store; atomically read and delete the nonce when the POST arrives
Blocker
Storing the expected nonce directly in a client cookie lets an attacker replay the cookie and token together (quote: "never store the expected nonce value directly inside a client cookie where an attacker could replay both the cookie and token")
Outcome
Partial — Each nonce is single-use, back/forward navigation won't reuse a consumed nonce, and intercepted tokens can't be replayed (official claim)

The token is bound to the nonce; if the nonce also lives on the client, one captured pair works again and again. Keep the nonce on the server and delete it on use, and replay becomes pointless.

Support Same as mwg:verify-email-ownership; recorded as Limited

mwg:verify-email-ownership:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Verify tokens only on the server with established libraries, and check DNS delegation before trusting iss CorrectionPartialLimited Verify email address ownership · Tampering goes undetected
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Parse EVT~KB-JWT with SD-JWT/JOSE libraries such as @sd-jwt/core and jose; before trusting iss, check the _email-verification.<domain> DNS TXT delegation and that .well-known/email-verification reports the same issuer; verify the EVT signature and the KB-JWT nonce, audience, freshness, and algorithm allowlist
Blocker
Verifying tokens in client-side JavaScript can be trivially bypassed or forged and gives the relying party no trust guarantee (quote: "client-side checks can be trivially bypassed or forged and provide zero trust guarantee to the relying party.")
Outcome
Partial — The email step is skipped only after the whole cryptographic chain verifies on the server; any failure falls back to a traditional verification email (official claim)

The token is a JWT anyone can decode, and glancing at email_verified: true in the front end is easy, but anyone can forge that glance. The real check is on the server: signature, delegation, binding, all of them.

Support Same as mwg:verify-email-ownership; recorded as Limited

mwg:verify-email-ownership:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

When the EVT has no kid, trial each of the provider's keys CorrectionPartialLimited Verify email address ownership · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When jwtVerify throws ERR_JWKS_MULTIPLE_MATCHING_KEYS, iterate the candidate keys from the error with for await and verify against each; during the transition treat Ed25519 and EdDSA as equivalent for the KB-JWT algorithm
Blocker
Providers such as Gmail omit kid from the EVT header, and with multiple keys in the JWKS jose throws outright (quote: "When kid is absent and the JWKS contains multiple keys, jose throws ERR_JWKS_MULTIPLE_MATCHING_KEYS")
Outcome
Partial — Catching the error and trialing each key lets verification pass once the matching key is found (official claim)

Textbook verification code fails for the biggest email provider, because its tokens don't say which key signed them.

mwg:carousel-slide-effects:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Set color-scheme on a component so its whole subtree uses the other scheme New capabilityPartialNewly Component-specific light/dark themes · Theme colors don't match their context
Unlock · brainstormKeeping a component dark used to mean copying a color set while native controls lagged behind → now code blocks, video players, maps, and component previews on a light docs site can each lock their scheme, scrollbars and checkboxes included, with no second stylesheet
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
With light-dark() color tokens on the page, set color-scheme: dark (or only dark to force it) on self-contained surfaces like code blocks, media players, galleries, and single-scheme embeds; tokens, form controls, and scrollbars in the subtree resolve under that scheme; skip shallow containers that just swap colors, like buttons
Blocker
The guide only lists use cases and says older browsers should switch custom properties on the root with prefers-color-scheme and avoid the media query on individual components; it doesn't say when the old approach breaks (quote: "Avoid using the media query on individual components unless the component requires a very specific type of dark mode customization beyond colors.")
Outcome
Partial — One color-scheme line switches the subtree and built-in browser UI to the other scheme; light-dark() is Newly, so unsupported browsers can only switch the whole page from the root with a media query (official claim)

A dark code block on a light page used to need its own set of colors, and its scrollbar stayed light anyway. Now you declare the scheme on that block and the tokens re-resolve themselves.

Support color-scheme: Widely available (2022-02-03); light-dark(): Newly available (2024-05-13), Supported by: Chrome 123, Edge 123, Firefox 120, Safari 17.5

mwg:component-specific-light-dark-theme · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Re-specify inherited color properties on elements that change color-scheme CorrectionPartialNewly Component-specific light/dark themes · Theme colors don't match their context
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
On the element with the color-scheme override, set color, accent-color, and other inherited color properties again (even to the same token); don't register design tokens as <color> with @property; don't use inherit on color properties of such elements
Blocker
light-dark() resolves at computed-value time, so only one of the two colors is inherited; when a component turns dark, its subtree's accent-color stays the light-scheme value (quote: "any inherited <color> properties set to a light-dark() color will only pass down one of the two colors to their descendants")
Outcome
Partial — Re-specified properties resolve again under the new scheme; unregistered tokens keep the live light-dark() expression for descendants to re-resolve (official claim)

The scheme switched and the background went dark, but the accent color is still the blue meant for a light background. What got inherited was the already-computed color, not the expression that depends on the scheme.

Support light-dark(): Newly

mwg:component-specific-light-dark-theme:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't set color-scheme on elements without a background CorrectionWinWidely Component-specific light/dark themes · Theme colors don't match their context
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Override color-scheme only on complex surfaces that have their own background and establish their own visual context
Blocker
Setting color-scheme on an element without a background mixes background and text colors from different schemes, making text unreadable (quote: "Do not set color-scheme on elements without a background, as that risks mixing background and text color pairs from different color-schemes, resulting in unreadable text.")
Outcome
Win — With its own background, the element's text and background come from the same scheme (official claim)

The text follows the element into dark mode and turns light, while the page's light background shows through: white on white.

mwg:component-specific-light-dark-theme:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Safari doesn't pass the embedder's scheme into iframes, so pass it explicitly CorrectionPartialWidely Component-specific light/dark themes · Theme colors don't match their context
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
If you control both sides, pass the parent's scheme to the iframe via a URL parameter (?theme=dark) or postMessage(), and have the iframe set a class or color-scheme on <html> from it; if you only control the embedded page, expose and document an explicit theme parameter in the embed API
Blocker
Safari doesn't render iframes per the embedder's color-scheme, and prefers-color-scheme inside the iframe still follows the system setting (quote: "Safari does not, and resolves prefers-color-scheme to the system setting even inside iframes.")
Outcome
Partial — Passing it explicitly keeps iframes in every browser in step with the parent; if you only control the embedded page, Safari offers no reliable detection and you depend on the embedder passing the parameter (official claim)

Chrome and Firefox let the iframe follow the outer scheme; Safari doesn't. A bright white widget sits inside a dark page, and the only fix is for both sides to talk through parameters.

mwg:component-specific-light-dark-theme:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Treat icons as CSS custom properties and let the cascade pick which one shows New capabilityPartialNewly Using and managing icons · Icon styling can't reach the icon
Unlock · brainstormIcon color and state used to need filters, file swaps, or class toggles → now a design system can ship its whole icon set as tokens: external links get an external-link icon automatically, expand buttons swap arrows by aria-expanded, and a toolbar resizes and recolors all its icons in one place, with no HTML changes
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Each icon is a custom property holding an image (--icon-trash); an element shows one by setting --icon-start/--icon-end; @container style(--icon-start) detects a value and draws it in ::before with mask and currentColor; state changes are just property overrides ([aria-expanded="true"] swaps the icon); size, color, and spacing come from inheritable --icon-style-* set on an ancestor
Blocker
Recoloring icons with <img> plus CSS filters can't reliably apply an arbitrary CSS color, so recoloring is cumbersome and inflexible (quote: "Filters can't reliably apply an arbitrary CSS color value to the image, so recoloring is cumbersome and inflexible.")
Outcome
Partial — Icons follow the text color and its transitions, one selector gives every matching element its icon, and there's no second element to show or hide and no class to toggle; container style queries only became Baseline in 2026-05, so older browsers fall back to class hooks with an image-set() trick (official claim)

An icon used to be another image: filter it or swap the file to change color, hide one and show another to change state. Once an icon is a property, it goes through the cascade like color or font size.

Support Container style queries: Newly available (2026-05-19), Supported by: Chrome 111, Edge 111, Firefox 151, Safari 18; Registered custom properties: Newly available (2024-07-09)

mwg:icons · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Register icon properties as non-inheriting CorrectionPartialNewly Using and managing icons · Cascade overrides don't match intent
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
@property --icon-start { syntax: "*"; inherits: false; } (same for --icon-end), forwarding the value to the pseudo-element with --icon-start: inherit inside the style query; without @property, reset descendants to initial inside the query
Blocker
If not registered as non-inheriting, an icon set on a button inherits down and every descendant draws one too (quote: "without it, an icon set on a button would inherit into every descendant, and each of them would render the icon too.")
Outcome
Partial — The icon appears only on the element that sets it (official claim)

Custom properties inherit by default, which is great for tokens and a disaster for this element has an icon: the span inside the button thinks it should draw one too.

Support Registered custom properties: Newly

mwg:icons:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use root-relative URLs or data URIs for icon tokens CorrectionPartialNewly Using and managing icons · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use root-relative paths (url("/icons/trash.svg")) or data URIs in icon tokens; if relative URLs are unavoidable, register with a syntax starting with <url> and declare the actual URL on :root; with many icons, register them in bulk via CSS.registerProperty
Blocker
A relative URL used through a custom property may resolve against the stylesheet that uses it rather than the one that defines it, pointing to the wrong file when they live in different directories; URLs in a registered property's initial-value only work in Firefox (quote: "If those stylesheets are in different directories, the URL may resolve to the wrong file.")
Outcome
Partial — Root-relative URLs and data URIs work everywhere; a <url>-first registration resolves against the declaring stylesheet in every browser, at the cost of declaring each icon twice and not working in shadow roots (official claim)

mwg:icons:6 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Leave custom-property hooks in symbols so <use> icons can be tuned from outside New capabilityPartialLimited Using and managing icons · Icon styling can't reach the icon
Unlock · brainstorm<use> icons used to allow little more than recoloring → now one symbol can change stroke width or even its path through CSS variables: play/pause or check/cross share one <use> and change shape with state. Path changes don't work in Safari yet
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
In symbols you control, add hooks such as fill="var(--icon-accent, currentColor)" and stroke-width="var(--icon-stroke, 2)", even CSS d: var(--icon-path, path(…)) on a path; inline the SVG when you don't control the source
Blocker
Content referenced by <use> is cloned into a shadow tree, so page CSS can't select its elements (quote: "With <use>, the referenced content is cloned into a shadow tree, so page CSS cannot select its elements.")
Outcome

<use> is cheap, at the price of page styles not reaching inside. As long as the icon leaves variables in place, you can still tune color and stroke from outside, and in most browsers even the shape.

mwg:icons:7 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Patch around two Safari rendering bugs CorrectionPartialNewly Using and managing icons · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts

Action + object
Under a Safari-only @supports, apply an empty keyframe animation to ::before/::after inside an @layer; in Safari re-register --icon-start as inheriting and reset descendants to initial inside the style query
Blocker
A WebKit bug can keep icons from appearing until the user interacts; another keeps a direct override of --icon-start on hover from taking effect (quote: "can prevent them from appearing until user interaction")
Outcome
Partial — The patch handles both display problems; living in a layer lets unlayered icon declarations still win (official claim)

The same icon set works in Chrome, but in Safari it only shows up after a click on the page. The bug is the browser's, but the page has to route around it.

Support Container style queries, registered custom properties: Newly

mwg:icons:8 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Flip the arrow along with the tooltip using anchored container queries New capabilityStalemateLimited Position-aware tooltips · Popups detach from their trigger
Unlock · brainstormAfter flipping, tooltip arrows used to point the wrong way, so people dropped the arrow or detected it in JS → now help bubbles, onboarding callouts, and chart annotations can keep the arrow pointing at the trigger in pure CSS. Chromium only for now
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Build the tooltip with popover and popovertarget (implicit anchor), set position-try-fallbacks: flip-block and container-type: anchored; inside @container anchored(fallback: flip-block) change the direction and position of the inner element's ::before arrow; add an inner wrapper to restyle the box itself
Blocker
The tooltip sits above its anchor with a down arrow; after scrolling it flips below, and the arrow points the wrong way on the wrong side (quote: "If the user scrolls and the tooltip flips to appear *below* the anchor, the arrow is now pointing the wrong way and is on the wrong side of the tooltip.")
Outcome
Stalemate — In supporting browsers the arrow follows the actual position; anchored container queries are only in Chrome/Edge 143 and nothing else can tell which fallback is active, so unsupported browsers can only hide the arrow with @supports (container-type: anchored) (official claim)

Anchor positioning makes tooltips flip at the edges, but only the browser knew it had flipped, not the arrow. The new query lets styles ask which side am I on now.

Support Anchor position container queries: Limited availability. Supported by: Chrome 143, Edge 143. Unsupported in: Firefox, Safari. Popover: Newly (2025-01-27). This guide gives no anchor positioning group status

Action + object

Blocker

Build a reading progress bar with a scroll() timeline New capabilityPartialNewly Scroll progress indicator · Scroll-linked effects rely on JS listeners
Unlock · brainstormReading progress bars used to need a scroll listener → now long articles, tutorials, and terms pages can add one in pure CSS, and each scrollable block on a page (code, chat logs) can carry its own

A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Give a fixed top element a scaleX(0)→scaleX(1) keyframe with animation-timeline: scroll() (tracking the nearest ancestor scroller, here the root) or a named scroll-timeline on :root; wrap in prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference and @supports (animation-timeline: scroll())

A progress bar is just how far have you scrolled, which used to be recomputed on every scroll. Now scroll position drives the animation itself.

mwg:scrollability-affordance-hints · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Mask only the two ends, and keep focused items out of the fade CorrectionPartialNewly Scrollability affordance hints · Focus or state indicators disappear
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Write the mask as an opaque layer minus two gradient bands, not a single gradient; when the scroller snaps or contains focusable items, set scroll-padding-inline (or -block) to the fade size
Blocker
A single gradient clipped to the border box hides the scroller's focus outline, and focused or snapped items can rest inside the fade (quote: "including a focus outline on the scroller, which a single gradient clipped to the border box would hide.")
Outcome

Action + object

Blocker

mwg:shrinking-header-on-scroll · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
With a sticky header, set scroll-padding-top on the scroller to the header height (animatable on the same scroll timeline); add padding-top equal to the header's initial height to body or the main content

mwg:shrinking-header-on-scroll:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

With percentage ranges, the shrinking element must be out of flow CorrectionPartialNewly Shrinking header on scroll · Layout depends on the wrong measurement
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When animation-range uses percentages, don't animate a position: static or relative element (the example uses position: fixed), or use pixel ranges
Blocker
An in-flow element shrinking also shrinks the total scroll distance, so percentage-based positions shift (quote: "Shrinking those elements as you scroll, would shrink the total scroll distance, thereby affecting the computed value of — for example — 10% into the scroll.")
Outcome

The animation advances by percent scrolled while the animation itself changes the total length, and the two tug at each other.

mwg:shrinking-header-on-scroll:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Truncate visually with CSS instead of cutting strings in script CorrectionWinWidely Single-line text overflow · Truncation hurts accessibility
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When a single-line label (tab, table cell, file name) is too long, truncate only on screen with text-overflow: ellipsis or a mask fade, keeping the full text in the DOM
Blocker
Cutting the string in script means screen readers, find-in-page and copy all get the mangled text (quote: "Screen readers announce the full text, find-in-page matches it, and copying copies it. This is why the string must never be cut in script.")
Outcome
Win — Truncation happens only at paint time; assistive tech, search and copy still get the full text. Ellipsis and masks are Widely available (official claim)

The common approach is to cut the string to N characters before rendering and append "…", which removes the full text from the DOM. The guide requires truncation to be visual only; to keep a tail such as a file extension, split it in the markup rather than cutting characters. Another trap: a single gradient mask also hides the focus outline ("it also hides the element's focus outline").

mwg:single-line-overflow · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Make truncated text reachable for keyboard and touch too New capabilityPartialNewly Single-line text overflow · Only reachable by hover
Unlock · brainstormTruncated long labels (file names, tab names) can scroll to reveal the full text on hover or focus in pure CSS, with no JS width measuring and no title.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Scroll the text into view on :hover and :focus-visible, with scroll-driven animations controlling the fade and hint
Blocker
Relying on the title attribute alone to show the full text leaves keyboard and touch users without it (quote: "DO NOT rely on title alone: it is unreachable from the keyboard and on touch.")
Outcome
Partial — Keyboard users see the full text via focus, but touch still has no hover or focus, so the guide asks for another route (the selected item is never truncated, a detail view opens). Scroll-driven animations only just became Newly available (official claim)

title is the easiest option but only works with a mouse. The new approach scrolls the text inside its box on hover and focus, shrinking the fade as it scrolls so the end is readable. The guide also admits "Hover and focus reveal nothing on touch", so on touch the opponent is sidestepped by interface design rather than defeated by the feature. Without scroll-driven animations, the fade and hint never appear and the text is clipped with no indication; a ResizeObserver fallback is needed.

mwg:single-line-overflow:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Restyle a sticky header when it's stuck with scroll-state(stuck: top) New capabilityStalemateLimited State-aware sticky headers · Scroll-linked effects rely on JS listeners
Unlock · brainstormRestyle when stuck used to need listeners or observers toggling classes → now section headings in long documents and group headers in tables can gain a background, shadow, or compact form when stuck, making it obvious which section you're in. Chromium only for now
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Set container-type: scroll-state and a container-name on the position: sticky element itself (not its scrolling ancestor); inside @container section-header scroll-state(stuck: top) change children's background, text color, and shadow (a container can't style itself from its own query); use stuck: block-start for other writing modes
Blocker

A stuck header overlaps content and often needs a background or shadow to stay readable. Is it stuck yet used to be inferred by script; the new query lets styles know directly.

mwg:state-aware-sticky-headers · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

To resize when stuck, turn off scroll anchoring on the parent CorrectionPartialLimited State-aware sticky headers · Jitter during interaction
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Set overflow-anchor: none on the sticky element's direct parent (not :root or body); avoid box-model changes in the stuck state for bottom-stuck elements
Blocker
Changing height, padding, or font size when stuck makes scroll anchoring on the in-flow content below compensate, pushing the element out of its stuck position and causing a flickering oscillation (quote: "Scroll anchoring on the in-flow content below the sticky element adjusts the scroll offset to compensate for the layout change, which pushes the element back out of its stuck position and triggers an oscillation.")
Outcome
Partial — With anchoring off on the parent, the stuck state can freely change box model and transforms; it has no effect for bottom-stuck elements (official claim)

The header shrinks as it sticks, content moves up to fill the gap, the browser scrolls back to keep things steady, the header unsticks and grows, and then sticks again.

Wherever a keyboard user tabs to, the stuck header is sitting right on top of it.

mwg:state-aware-sticky-headers:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Flip panels at viewport edges with position-area plus position-try-fallbacks New capabilityPartialNewly Viewport-aware dropdowns · Layout unexpectedly overflows or shifts
Unlock · brainstormCollision handling used to require a positioning library → now row-end more actions menus, card corner menus, and footer language pickers can open upward or leftward near viewport edges using CSS alone
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Open a popover="auto" panel from a button with popovertarget (implicit anchor, no anchor-name needed); give the panel position-area: block-end span-inline-end and inset: auto, plus position-try-fallbacks: flip-block, flip-inline, flip-block flip-inline; don't mix logical and physical keywords
Blocker
When the default position overflows, the panel gets cut off at the screen edge; this used to take JS libraries like Popper.js or Floating UI to compute position and collisions (quote: "To prevent the panel from being cut off at the edge of the screen, define "try tactics" that the browser should attempt if the default position overflows.")
Outcome
Partial — The browser tries each flip in turn, keeping the panel inside the viewport and attached to its trigger; core anchor positioning properties are Newly, and older browsers need the @oddbird/css-anchor-positioning polyfill loaded conditionally or an @supports gate that keeps default centering (official claim; support status corrected per verified deviation)

The more menu on the last table row opens downward and runs off screen. Instead of a positioning library measuring all the time, list a few fallbacks and let the browser pick one that fits.

Support Anchor positioning core properties: Newly (2026-01-13). The guide says "Supported by: Safari 27 (Sep 2026). Unsupported in: Chrome, Edge, and Firefox."; that group status is misleading, see lines/ui-components/app-menus.md; this line doesn't use position-visibility. Popover: Newly (2025-01-27)

mwg:viewport-aware-dropdowns · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Without a polyfill, put inset: auto and margin: 0 inside @supports CorrectionPartialNewly Viewport-aware dropdowns · Popups detach from their trigger
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Put inset: auto and margin: 0 together with the positioning rules inside @supports (position-area: block-end); optionally give a bottom-fixed panel under @supports not (...)
Blocker
Setting inset: auto or margin: 0 unconditionally cancels the popover's default centering in browsers without anchor positioning while position-area is ignored, stranding the popover at (0, 0) or over its trigger (quote: "leaving the popover stranded at (0, 0) or overlapping its trigger.")
Outcome
Partial — Unsupported browsers keep default centering (or a bottom panel) instead of a top-left corner popover (official claim)

Resetting inset is meant to let anchor positioning take over, but in browsers without it nothing takes over and the panel drops into the top-left corner.

Support Same as mwg:viewport-aware-dropdowns; recorded as Newly

mwg:viewport-aware-dropdowns:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't wrap rules meant for the anchor positioning polyfill in @supports CorrectionPartialNewly Viewport-aware dropdowns · Feature detection is unreliable
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Load the polyfill only when CSS.supports("anchor-name: --a") is false; leave polyfilled rules outside @supports; give each trigger/panel pair explicit matching anchor-name/position-anchor, and use anchor() on insets instead of position-area when fallbacks are needed
Blocker
Unsupported browsers evaluate @supports (position-area: ...) and @supports (anchor-name: --a) as false and discard the whole block before the polyfill can process it (quote: "Browsers without native anchor positioning evaluate @supports (position-area: ...) and @supports (anchor-name: --a) to false and discard the block before the polyfill can process it.")
Outcome
Partial — Rules stay in the stylesheet for the polyfill to read; but the polyfill doesn't support implicit anchors or position-area with fallbacks, so the CSS has to bend to it (official claim)

@supports is meant to protect old browsers, but with a polyfill it hides the very rules the polyfill needs to read.

Support Same as mwg:viewport-aware-dropdowns; recorded as Newly

mwg:viewport-aware-dropdowns:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't add role="menu" or aria-haspopup without the full keyboard contract CorrectionWinN/A Viewport-aware dropdowns · Semantic structure misrepresents the page
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Treat the panel as a button group inside a popover; add role="menu"/menuitem and aria-haspopup only if the full keyboard contract such as arrow-key navigation is implemented
Blocker
Adding aria-haspopup to the trigger promises assistive tech a matching role the target doesn't have in this pattern (quote: "its value (menu, listbox, tree, grid, or the legacy true) is a promise that the target exposes a matching role, which this pattern does not.")
Outcome
Win — Declared roles match actual behavior, so screen reader users don't expect menu interactions that don't exist (official claim)

When the screen reader says menu, users press arrow keys, and nothing moves. A role is a promise; don't make one you can't keep.

Support ARIA: the guide gives no support data; recorded as N/A

mwg:viewport-aware-dropdowns:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Turn off axis locking for 2D panning with scroll-axis-lock: none New capabilityStalemateLimited Two-dimensional panning · Native scroll gestures don't fit the use
Unlock · brainstormFree diagonal panning used to mean giving up native scrolling and writing a drag engine → now whiteboards, mind maps, large spreadsheets, and floor-plan viewers can pan freely in a native scroll container, with the browser's scrollbars, keyboard, and momentum. Chrome 153 only for now
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Set scroll-axis-lock: none on the element that establishes the 2D scroll container (overflow: auto) itself; it doesn't inherit, so a wrapper has no effect and nested 1D panels keep the default auto; keep auto on articles, feeds, and 1D lists
Blocker
If a gesture starts mostly along one axis, the browser locks scrolling to that axis and ignores perpendicular deltas, often for the rest of the gesture, so diagonal or L-shaped panning on maps, zoomed images, infinite canvases, and node graphs gets stuck (quote: "the browser locks the scroll to that single horizontal or vertical axis and ignores perpendicular deltas—often for the remainder of the gesture.")
Outcome
Stalemate — With support you keep compositor scrolling, momentum, native scrollbars, and keyboard navigation without axis locking; only Chrome 153, so elsewhere you either accept locking or use a JS fallback with wheel plus pointermove writing scrollLeft/scrollTop, moving scrolling to the main thread and losing touch momentum after release (official claim)

Axis locking stops articles from drifting sideways, but on a map it means you drag toward the bottom-right and the view only goes right. You used to write your own panning engine; now one property tells the browser this area is two-dimensional.

Support scroll-axis-lock: Limited availability. Supported by: Chrome 153. Unsupported in: Edge, Firefox, Safari

mwg:2d-panning · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Action + object
Add overscroll-behavior: none or contain to the 2D scroll container
Blocker

Outcome
Win — Panning stops at the container edge without dragging the page or accidentally leaving it (official claim)

Drag the map all the way left and the browser decides you want to go back a page.

mwg:2d-panning:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Pan with scrollLeft/scrollTop, not transform CorrectionWinN/A Two-dimensional panning · Hand-built controls lose native behavior
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When handling panning yourself, add the deltas to scrollLeft and scrollTop
Blocker

Outcome

Dragging a canvas with transform feels natural, but from that moment the browser no longer knows this area can scroll.

Slide the tab underline to the newly active tab with anchor positioning New capabilityPartialNewly Anchor positioning tab underline · Size changes jump instead of animating
Unlock · brainstormSliding active indicators used to need JS measurement → now tab bars, segmented controls, and vertical sidebars can slide the indicator from old position to new in pure CSS, width and all; the same trick could make a highlight box that follows the hovered row
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Draw the underline in the <ul>'s ::before with position: absolute and position-anchor: --active; set anchor-name: --active on the active <li>; position with inset properties and anchor() (inset-inline-start: anchor(left) and so on), adding transition: inset .2s under prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference

Sliding an underline from one tab to another used to mean measuring both tabs and pushing it over with JS. Now the underline just follows whichever element holds that anchor name; when the name moves, so does the line.

mwg:anchor-positioning-tab-underline:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Transition elements appearing and display: none with @starting-style and allow-discrete New capabilityPartialLimited Animate element entry and exit · Size changes jump instead of animating
Unlock · brainstormToggling hidden used to be instant, and animating meant JS adding a class, waiting a frame, then adding an animation class → now notifications, expanded help text, and filtered result cards can fade in and out just by toggling hidden. Firefox exits still need the JS fallback
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Put final values in the base styles; put the entry starting point in @starting-style; list display in transition and add a separate transition-behavior: allow-discrete; put the exit end state on [hidden]; elements inserted with appendChild() also enter through @starting-style

Toggle the hidden attribute and the element just pops in or out, transitions or not. Now entry has a starting point and exit can keep display until the animation ends, though Firefox exits still need a script.

mwg:animate-element-entry-exit · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't rely on CSS.supports() alone to know if display can transition CorrectionPartialLimited Animate element entry and exit · Feature detection is unreliable
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Create a temporary probe element with transition: display 1s allow-discrete, set it to display: none, and check whether the computed value is still block; if not supported, take the data-closing fallback
Blocker
Firefox 129+ parses transition-behavior: allow-discrete (CSS.supports() returns true) but doesn't actually transition display, so elements vanish immediately on exit (quote: "Firefox 129+ parses transition-behavior: allow-discrete (CSS.supports('transition-behavior', 'allow-discrete') returns true) without actually transitioning the display property")
Outcome

Feature detection says supported, and the user sees the element simply vanish. This time the browser knows the syntax but doesn't do what the syntax promises.

Blocker

mwg:animate-element-entry-exit:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Trigger the exit first and wait for animations to finish before remove() CorrectionPartialNewly Animate element entry and exit · Size changes jump instead of animating
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Set element.hidden = true to start the exit, then Promise.race between Promise.allSettled(getAnimations().map(a => a.finished)) and a 2-second safety timeout, and only then call element.remove()
Blocker
element.remove() takes effect immediately and doesn't trigger a CSS transition (quote: "Since element.remove() is instantaneous and doesn't trigger a CSS transition on its own")
Outcome

The fade-out written for dismissing a notification never shows up, because the node is gone before the animation starts.

mwg:animate-element-entry-exit:4 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Animate dialogs and popovers into and out of the top layer with overlay and allow-discrete New capabilityPartialLimited Animate elements to and from the top layer · Size changes jump instead of animating
Unlock · brainstormWanting an animated dialog used to mean abandoning native <dialog> → now confirm dialogs, side drawers, and floating menus can keep native focus handling, Esc to close, and top-layer behavior while fading, scaling, and dimming the backdrop. Native exit animation is Chromium-only for now
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Put closed values on the base dialog/[popover] styles, list display and overlay in transition-property with a separate transition-behavior: allow-discrete; place @starting-style inside (or after) the open-state selector; transition ::backdrop the same way; apply scaling only under prefers-reduced-motion: no-preference
Blocker
Top-layer elements toggle between display: none and visible and have long been hard to animate; without transitioning overlay, an exiting element leaves the top layer early and gets clipped by other elements or the viewport (quote: "This ensures the element stays in the top layer for the duration of the animation, preventing it from being clipped by other elements or the viewport prematurely.")
Outcome
Partial — Entry works in pure CSS wherever @starting-style is supported; exit needs overlay (Chrome/Edge only) and display transitions, so Firefox and Safari need a data-closing plus wait-for-animations JS fallback, and popover light dismiss and popovertarget toggles can't be intercepted there and still close instantly (official claim)

Native <dialog> opened and closed instantly, so many sites switched to custom modals. Now opening can fade and scale with a dimming backdrop, and closing can stay in the top layer until the animation ends, though only Chromium does the exit natively for now.

Support @starting-style: Newly (2024-08-06); overlay: Limited availability, Supported by: Chrome 117, Edge 117, Unsupported in: Firefox, Safari; transitionable display: Limited (Chrome 117, Edge 117, Safari 18); Popover: Newly

mwg:animate-to-from-top-layer:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Move the real DOM nodes instead of faking order with CSS New capabilityPartialLimited Reordering with drag and drop · Visual order drifts from DOM order
Unlock · brainstormA playing video, a live iframe widget or a half-filled form can be moved elsewhere without resetting. Previously every move reloaded it, which made a rearrangeable live dashboard painful.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
On drop, move the existing node once with container.moveBefore(item, ref) so visual, keyboard and reading order stay aligned
Blocker
Changing on-screen order with CSS order or flex-direction: *-reverse leaves DOM order unchanged, so Tab and screen readers follow the old order (quote: "Reorder the real children in the DOM (not via CSS order or flex-direction: *-reverse) so visual, keyboard, and reading order stay aligned.")
Outcome
Partial — Moving real nodes fixes the order mismatch, but keeping internal state needs moveBefore(), which Safari lacks; the before() fallback loses focus and other state inside items (official claim)

The cheapest drag-to-reorder is changing order: it looks right, but keyboard users jump through the old order. The guide moves the real node once on release and uses moveBefore() to keep focus and iframe state. When visual and DOM order truly cannot match in a complex layout, reading-flow is the tool, but only Chrome/Edge support it.

Support moveBefore(): Limited (Chrome 133, Edge 133, Firefox 144; not Safari); reading-flow: Limited (Chrome/Edge 137; not Firefox or Safari)

mwg:drag-and-drop · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Show a drop marker while dragging; move only on release CorrectionWinWidely Reordering with drag and drop · Jitter during interaction
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Keep the source item in its slot during the drag, draw a drop marker with ::before/::after, and move on pointerup
Blocker
Reordering live while dragging makes siblings shift and the list jitter (quote: "Don't reorder while the pointer moves: shifting siblings feel jittery.")
Outcome
Win — A drop marker replaces live shifting and the jitter is gone; pointer capture and fixed positioning are mature features (official claim)

Many drag implementations reorder on pointermove, so the whole list jumps every time the pointer passes an item. The guide shows only a drop line during the drag, moves once on release, and animates every move so items travel instead of jumping.

Support Pointer events: Widely

mwg:drag-and-drop:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Sort by moving existing nodes, not rebuilding with innerHTML New capabilityPartialLimited User-triggered dynamic sorting · Rebuilding the DOM loses state
Unlock · brainstormAfter sorting or filtering, the list animates items to their new places with a view transition while scroll, focus and playback inside each item survive. Suits lists of heavy items (embedded previews, video cards).
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
When the user changes sort order, move the existing nodes into the new order with moveBefore(), optionally wrapped in a view transition
Blocker
Creating new nodes with innerHTML or a full re-render loses focus, scroll position inside items and image state, and breaks view transitions (quote: "Recreating nodes with innerHTML or a re-render that replaces them discards focus, scroll position inside items, and image state, and it breaks view transitions, which match old and new elements by identity.")
Outcome
Partial — Moving nodes alone stops focus and scroll loss. moveBefore() is missing in Safari; the append() fallback keeps order but may lose iframe, animation or inner focus state. View transitions are Newly (official claim)

Redrawing the whole list after sorting is the usual approach, and the cost is that the button the user just clicked loses focus and a half-scrolled card jumps to the top. The guide turns sorting into "moving house" rather than "rebuilding", with animation as an optional enhancement.

Support moveBefore(): Limited (not Safari); View transitions: Newly (2025-10-14)

mwg:dynamic-sort · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Announce the result once through a single role="status" region and keep focus on the control that triggered the sort
Blocker

A reorder moves many nodes, but the user did one thing. With aria-live on the list, screen readers treat every node move as an update. A single status region announcing once is enough.

When a <button> in a column header is clicked, move the existing <tr> elements within <tbody> and mark the sorted column with aria-sort
Blocker

This is the table version of dynamic-sort. mwg:sortable-table:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

mwg:accordion · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Avoid exclusive <details name> so content doesn't jump out of view CorrectionWinNewly Accordion · Side effects of a new feature
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Allow several items open at once by default; don't use the exclusive behaviour of <details name>
Blocker
In an exclusive accordion, opening one item closes another and content shifts out of view, which seriously affects screen-magnifier users (quote: "They also can cause content to shift out of the user’s view which can cause significant problems for those using screen magnifiers.")
Outcome
Win — Without exclusivity there is no such shift; the guide takes a clear stance where the new feature itself is the opponent (official claim)

<details name> only became Baseline in 2024, yet the guide advises against it. What makes this line unusual is that the opponent is not an old technique but a side effect of a new feature. When exclusivity is truly needed, the guide says to use it only as a last resort.

mwg:accordion:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Collapse with ::details-content to keep find-in-page and animation New capabilityPartialNewly Accordion · Hiding or disabling locks people out
Unlock · brainstormA long page can be fully collapsed while Ctrl+F still finds and expands matches, with an opening animation. Long docs, FAQs and weekly notes no longer have to choose between collapsing and being findable.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Collapsed content lives in ::details-content and is hidden with content-visibility: hidden rather than display: none
Blocker
Hiding collapsed content with display: none makes it unfindable by find-in-page and its height cannot animate (quote: "While closed it is hidden with content-visibility: hidden, not display: none, which is what keeps it findable and lets its size animate.")
Outcome
Partial — In supporting browsers content is searchable and slides open; ::details-content is only Newly, so older browsers get no animation (official claim)

Once a collapsed section is display: none, Ctrl+F cannot find it. A closed <details> hides its content with content-visibility: hidden instead, so the content is still there and searchable, and the browser opens it automatically on a search hit. The ::details-content pseudo-element is what lets you select, style and animate the height of the collapsed part. Conversely, use display: none deliberately when the content should not be searchable.

Support <details> auto-opens on find-in-page match: Chrome 97, Firefox 148, Safari 26.2 (Safari does not scroll to the matched text correctly, WebKit bug 304174; not mapped to any web-features feature, so no Baseline label); ::details-content: Newly (2025-09-16); transition-behavior: Newly (2024-08-06)

mwg:accordion:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Pin menus to their trigger with anchor positioning New capabilityPartialNewly Application menu bar · Popups detach from their trigger
Unlock · brainstormA desktop-style File/Edit/View menu bar can be built almost declaratively with popover plus anchor positioning, with menus attached to their buttons. Web tools can look and behave like desktop programs.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Each menu in a menu bar (File/Edit/View) is a popover placed next to its trigger with CSS anchor positioning
Blocker
Without anchor positioning a popover falls back to the centered viewport position, detaching the menu from its trigger, and a centered submenu covers its parent (quote: "Never ship that for a menu: it detaches the menu from its trigger, and a centered submenu covers its parent.")
Outcome
Partial — Menus stay attached to their triggers. The core anchor properties (anchor-name, position-area, anchor()) are supported in Chrome 125, Firefox 147 and Safari 26 (Baseline Newly, 2026-01-13); older browsers still need an @supports gate (official claim; support corrected after verification on 2026-11-12)

A menu bar is one widget: one Tab stop, left/right arrows switch menus, focus previews. Each menu must sit under its trigger, but a native popover defaults to the middle of the viewport. The guide's support section says anchor positioning is "supported only in Safari 27", but that is the status of the whole feature group, not of the properties this guide uses; see the details.

Support Anchor positioning core properties: Newly (2026-01-13; position-anchor 2026-09-14). The guide says "Supported by: Safari 27. Unsupported in: Chrome, Edge, and Firefox.", which is the lowest common support of the whole web-features anchor-positioning group (325 sub-features); Chrome and Firefox only lack the position-visibility: anchor-valid / anchor-visible values (Chrome 157 beta, not Firefox), which this guide does not use. Popover: Newly (2025-01-27); focusgroup: Limited (Chrome/Edge 150)

mwg:app-menus · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Keep separators so breadcrumbs read as a hierarchy, not siblings CorrectionWinWidely Breadcrumbs · Appearance implies the wrong structure
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Build breadcrumbs with <nav> + <ol>, with separators generated in CSS and hidden from assistive tech
Blocker
Pill or segmented styles without separators look like tabs or filters (items on the same level), not a hierarchy (quote: "Pills, tags and a segmented bar without one look like tabs or filters (items on the same level), not a hierarchy.")
Outcome
Win — Separators are CSS-generated content and work in every browser (official claim)

Designers often want breadcrumbs to look more like a button row, and the result implies the wrong structure. The guide treats separators as essential and uses ol rather than ul to express order.

mwg:breadcrumbs · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Use border-shape so borders and focus rings follow slanted segments New capabilityStalemateLimited Breadcrumbs · Focus or state indicators disappear
Unlock · brainstormSlanted, arrow or notched shapes can carry their borders and focus rings along the shape, without sacrificing accessibility to clip-path. Chromium only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Draw slanted or arrow-shaped segments with corner-shape / border-shape, with borders and focus outlines following the shape
Blocker
Cutting shapes with clip-path also clips borders and focus outlines; forced-colors mode removes the background, so a clipped segment loses its outline (quote: "clip-path clips borders and outlines, so under the fallback use a color/background change plus negative outline-offset for focus instead of an outside outline.")
Outcome
Stalemate — corner-shape and border-shape are Chrome/Edge only; other browsers fall back to rectangular segments with separators, and the guide says rounded corners are not an acceptable substitute for slants (official claim)

Arrow-shaped breadcrumbs used to rely on clip-path, so the keyboard focus ring was cut off and whole segments vanished in high-contrast mode. The new properties make the border itself the shape, but Firefox and Safari do not support them yet.

Support corner-shape: Limited (Chrome/Edge 139); border-shape: Limited (Chrome/Edge 147)

mwg:breadcrumbs:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Loop a carousel without cloning slides by parking one with order New capabilityPartialNewly Carousel · Duplicate content confuses assistive tech
Unlock · brainstormAn infinitely looping carousel without cloned slides: park one slide with CSS order and compensate the offset once scrolling settles. Content exists once, so frameworks and screen readers don't fight.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
For looping, use --current and CSS order to park the first or last slide beside the current one and mark it inert, without cloning or moving slides
Blocker
Cloning slides or moving them in the DOM for infinite looping conflicts with frameworks and makes assistive tech read duplicate content (quote: "Do not clone slides or move them in the DOM, which conflicts with frameworks and duplicates content for assistive technology.")
Outcome
Partial — No clones means no duplicate content, but the offset must be compensated when scrolling settles with snapping briefly turned off, and scrollend is only Newly (official claim)

The usual infinite carousel clones a slide at each end, so screen readers hear the same slide twice. The guide instead visually parks a single slide at the other end and makes it inert, so Tab order and the accessibility tree match what is on screen. The guide also says not to use ::scroll-button() / ::scroll-marker "for now", without giving a reason.

Support Scroll snap: Widely (2020-01-15); scrollend: Newly (2025-12-12); Scroll-driven animations: Newly (2026-10-27)

mwg:carousel · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Don't disable the button while showing feedback CorrectionWinWidely Copy button · Hiding or disabling locks people out
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
After copying, show feedback; on repeat clicks, reset the timer and restart the animation instead of setting the button to disabled
Blocker
Disabling the button during feedback drops keyboard focus and announces a misleading unavailable state (quote: "DO NOT disable the button while showing feedback: copying is idempotent, and disabling drops keyboard focus, announces a misleading unavailable state")
Outcome
Win — The button stays usable and focus stays put (official claim)

Disabling the button to prevent double clicks is an intuitive safeguard, but copying twice has no side effect. Disabling makes a keyboard user's next Tab start again from the top of the page.

Support Clipboard.writeText: Widely (2020-03-24)

The same recommendation appears in sortable-table and accordion: declarative attributes instead of click listeners. None of the three guides says where hand-wired listeners break; they only describe the new style. Both slot 3 and the outcome are missing on this line.

mwg:copy-button:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Hide the many repeated copy buttons on a page until hover or focus, using only opacity
Blocker

A copy button on every code block is noisy, so they are often shown only on hover. The catch is how they are hidden: most hiding methods also take the button out of the Tab order. The guide also forbids "hiding" with aria-hidden or inert.

mwg:copy-button:3 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

No hard-coded breakpoint: collapse only when it doesn't fit New capabilityPartialNewly Responsive site navigation · Layout depends on the wrong measurement
Unlock · brainstormA navbar collapses into a native popover when it doesn't fit, with keyboard, Esc and click-outside closing built in; changing the number of links or the language needs no breakpoint tuning.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Use one <nav> tree; script compares scrollWidth with clientWidth and collapses into a native popover only when the row doesn't fit
Blocker
With a hard-coded breakpoint, adding a link, switching language or enlarging text makes the nav overflow or collapse too early at the wrong width (quote: "There is no hard-coded breakpoint. Script collapses the navigation only when the inline row (brand, actions and every link) does not fit, so adding a link, a translation or larger text moves the point automatically.")
Outcome
Partial — The collapse point follows the actual content, but deciding still needs JS measurement; popover is Newly and older browsers need a polyfill (official claim)

Traditional navbars switch to a hamburger at a fixed media-query width, so after translation into a longer language the links overflow before the breakpoint. The guide measures whether the row actually fits, and defaults to the collapsed layout without script so it always works.

Support Popover: Newly (2025-01-27); @starting-style: Newly (2024-08-06)
