mwg:natural-interactions:2 · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Apply shaders directly to interactive HTML New capabilityStalemateLimited Applying WebGL shaders to HTML content · Hand-written interaction inside canvas
Unlock · brainstormA real button that ripples when pressed, or a real input with glass refraction or CRT scanlines: the effect is a GPU shader, yet you can still type, select text and reach it with a screen reader. Chrome 157+ only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Upload drawable HTML elements as a WebGL texture with texElementSubImage2D(), apply fragment/vertex shaders, then align hit testing with the drawn pixels using canvas.updateElementGeometry()
Blocker
(missing) The guide only says native accessibility, text selection and DOM events are retained after applying shaders, without saying what the previous approach was or where it broke (quote: "This enables applying high-performance fragment and vertex shaders natively to fully interactive UI components, such as buttons, input fields, and rich text, while retaining native accessibility, text selection, and DOM event handling.")
Outcome
Stalemate — The guide claims buttons, inputs and rich text stay interactive under a shader; but only Chrome 157+ supports it, and elsewhere the fallback is overlaying HTML on the canvas with CSS (so no shader effect) or the three-html-render polyfill (official claim)

Effects like ripple distortion, lighting and color grading only work on textures, and a texture is not an element. The new API turns a real element's painted output into a texture while the element itself stays in the DOM to receive input. The guide never describes the old approach, so the opponent is marked missing. You still have to allocate the texture with texImage2D(), reallocate only on size change, and handle captureElementImage() throwing before the first paint.

Support HTML-in-Canvas: Limited (Chrome 157+, stable since 2026-11-03; not Firefox or Safari). The guide's fallback section still says "HTML-in-canvas is not natively supported by any major browser yet.", which is out of date

mwg:apply-webgl-shaders · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Let the browser paint HTML into the canvas and export it, instead of a library redrawing the DOM New capabilityStalemateLimited Exporting HTML content from canvas · Re-implementing browser rendering by hand
Unlock · brainstormDashboards, styled documents and interactive charts can be saved as a PNG identical to the screen, or an HTML interaction can be recorded as video with captureStream(), without loading a screenshot library. Chrome 157+ only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Put the HTML to export inside <canvas content="drawable">, draw it with drawElementImage(), then export an image or video with toDataURL(), toBlob() or captureStream()
Blocker
The old approach relies on bulky third-party libraries that parse DOM nodes and CSS properties one by one to rebuild a facsimile on a canvas: expensive, error-prone and often missing modern CSS layout features (quote: "This approach is computationally expensive, error-prone, and frequently fails to support modern CSS layout features.")
Outcome
Stalemate — The browser's own rendering engine paints it, so the export matches the screen; but only Chrome 157+ supports it, and elsewhere the fallback is exactly html2canvas, dom-to-image or snapdom (rrweb for frame-by-frame capture), or a warning that export is unsupported (official claim)

Saving a piece of the page as an image has meant a library re-implementing the browser's layout and painting in JS, and getting it wrong whenever a new CSS feature shows up. The new API has the real engine paint the HTML subtree into a canvas, after which the existing canvas export methods work. Outside Chrome, the fallback is those same libraries, so the opponent stands untouched.

Support HTML-in-Canvas: Limited (Chrome 157+, stable since 2026-11-03; not Firefox or Safari). The guide's fallback section still says "HTML-in-canvas is not natively supported by any major browser yet.", which is out of date

mwg:export-html-media-from-canvas · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Draw real DOM into the canvas so its content is no longer invisible to the browser New capabilityStalemateLimited Exposing canvas content to browser features · Canvas content is invisible to the browser
Unlock · brainstormText drawn on a canvas (charts, whiteboards, map labels) can be found with Ctrl+F, translated and read by screen readers without maintaining a hidden DOM copy. Chrome 157+ only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Put real HTML children (marked drawable) inside <canvas content="drawable"> and draw them in onpaint with drawElementImage() (or copy them to a texture in WebGL/WebGPU)
Blocker
Content drawn on a regular <canvas> is invisible to screen readers, indexing, translation tools, find-in-page and print (quote: "Regular <canvas> content is not exposed to browser features such as screen readers, indexing, translation tools, accessibility assistive tools, find-in-page, print, etc.")
Outcome
Stalemate — The content stays in the DOM so browser features keep working, but only Chrome 157+ supports it; elsewhere you overlay HTML on the canvas with CSS or use the three-html-render polyfill (official claim)

A canvas has always been an opaque block of pixels: text on it cannot be selected, searched or translated, and assistive tech cannot read it. The new API keeps the canvas's children laid out and in the DOM tree while the canvas only decides how to paint them; in 2D the browser even aligns hit testing automatically. Cross-origin content cannot be drawn, and with Chrome-only support you have to maintain the fallback alongside it to cover other browsers.

Support HTML-in-Canvas: Limited (Chrome 157+, stable since 2026-11-03; not Firefox or Safari). The guide's fallback section still says "HTML-in-canvas is not natively supported by any major browser yet.", which is out of date

mwg:expose-canvas-content-to-browser-features · Upstream guide · Line file · v1.0.0

Project real DOM into a 3D scene and hand interaction back to the browser New capabilityStalemateLimited Interactive HTML content in 3D scenes · Hand-written interaction inside canvas
Unlock · brainstormScreens, boards and control panels inside a 3D scene can be real forms and buttons that accept typing, selection and keyboard focus, with no raycasting-to-event code. Good for product showcases or property panels in a 3D editor. Chrome 157+ only for now.
Who acts
A developer (or a coding agent)
Action + object
Copy drawable HTML elements into a texture (WebGL texElementSubImage2D(), WebGPU drawElementImageToTexture(), Three.js HTMLTexture), then align each element with the 3D image using canvas.updateElementGeometry()
Blocker
(missing) The guide only says complex raycasting or custom event handling is no longer required, without saying where the hand-written version fails (quote: "they retain full interactivity—allowing users to click buttons, select text, and trigger focus states natively without requiring complex raycasting or custom event handling")
Outcome
Stalemate — Elements stay in the DOM layout tree, so clicks, text selection and focus states are handled by the browser; but only Chrome 157+ supports it, and elsewhere the fallback is overlaying HTML on the canvas with CSS or the three-html-render polyfill (official claim)

A panel in a 3D scene used to be a texture: clicks had to be converted with raycasting and events dispatched by hand. With the new API a real element sits behind the texture, and the developer only converts the MVP matrix into a canvasTransform each frame to tell the browser where it was drawn. The opponent is described only as "complex": effort, not an observable failure. Matrix syncing is still manual, though Three.js's InteractionManager does it for you.

Support HTML-in-Canvas: Limited (Chrome 157+, stable since 2026-11-03; not Firefox or Safari). The guide's fallback section still says "HTML-in-canvas is not natively supported by any major browser yet.", which is out of date
